<div class="ra xbox p" role="complementary" aria-label="Related or updated information about this article"><div>
<strong>Previous version available:</strong> This article is based on a previously available preprint posted on bioRxiv on May 13, 2025: "<a href="/articles/PMC11383681/" class="usa-link">
Chemical crosslinking extends and complements UV crosslinking in analysis of RNA/DNA nucleic acid–protein interaction sites by mass spectrometry
</a>".</div></div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Ultraviolet (UV) crosslinking with mass spectrometry (XL-MS) has been established for identifying RNA- and DNA-binding proteins along with their domains and amino acids involved. Here, we explore chemical XL-MS for RNA–protein, DNA–protein, and nucleotide–protein complexes <em>in vitro</em> and <em>in vivo</em>. We introduce a specialized nucleotide–protein-crosslink search engine, NuXL, for robust and fast identification of such crosslinks at amino acid resolution. Chemical XL-MS complements UV XL-MS by generating different crosslink species, increasing crosslinked protein yields <em>in vivo</em> almost four-fold, and thus it expands the structural information accessible via XL-MS. Our workflow facilitates integrative structural modelling of nucleic acid–protein complexes and adds spatial information to the described RNA-binding properties of enzymes, for which crosslinking sites are often observed close to their cofactor-binding domains. <em>In vivo</em> UV and chemical XL-MS data from <em>E. coli</em> cells analysed by NuXL establish a comprehensive nucleic acid–protein crosslink inventory with crosslink sites at amino acid level for &gt;1500 proteins. Our new workflow combined with the dedicated NuXL search engine identified RNA crosslinks that cover most RNA-binding proteins, with DNA and RNA crosslinks detected in transcriptional repressors and activators.</p></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><h2>Graphical Abstract</h2>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ga1"><h3 class="obj_head">Graphical Abstract.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727figgra1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/646498836f12/gkaf727figgra1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="301" width="787" alt="Graphical Abstract"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ga1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></figure></section><section id="SEC1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Proteins and RNA (or DNA) form functional complexes termed ribonucleoproteins, RNPs (or deoxyribonucleoproteins, DNPs), which drive and organize key cellular processes. For studying RNA–protein interactions, ultraviolet (UV) crosslinking combined with mass spectrometry (XL-MS) has been established as a standard method, in which the identification of crosslinked RNA–protein heteroconjugates is based either on the inherent reactivity of pyrimidines (mainly uracil) or on UV-reactive derivatives, 4-thiouridine or 6-thioguanine [<a href="#B1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1">1–8</a>]. In earlier studies, we demonstrated that UV XL-MS can identify the sites of RNA/DNA–protein crosslinking at amino acid resolution and the compositions of the crosslinked (oligo)nucleotide adducts in samples of varying complexity [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>, <a href="#B9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9">9–12</a>]. Other compelling mass spectrometry (MS)-based studies of the RNA–protein interactome in cells have contributed to this research field by establishing dedicated workflows for UV XL-MS. RBDmap and pCLAP protocols have been used to identify RNA-binding proteins and binding domains by identification of peptides neighbouring crosslinked peptides using sequential proteolytic digestion [<a href="#B13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13">13</a>, <a href="#B14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14">14</a>]. Similarly, the CAPRI protocol identifies RNA-crosslinked proteins and peptides neighbouring crosslinked peptides [<a href="#B5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5">5</a>]. XRNAX and OOPS use UV crosslinking with organic phase separation to identify RNA-crosslinked proteins and peptides by MS [<a href="#B7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7">7</a>, <a href="#B8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8">8</a>]. TRAPP uses silica-based enrichment of UV-crosslinked proteins and for its variant iTRAPP, resolution is increased to the amino acid level [<a href="#B6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6">6</a>]. Using (p)RBS-ID protocol, Bae <em>et al.</em> established chemical digestion of the RNA in UV-crosslinked samples to generate mononucleosides, thus facilitating identification of crosslinked peptides by minimizing the database search space of the MS crosslinking data [<a href="#B3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3">3</a>, <a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">4</a>]. CLIR-MS identifies UV-crosslinked amino acids and the crosslinked nucleotide in the RNA sequence by using stable isotope-labelled RNA <em>in vitro</em> [<a href="#B15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15">15</a>].</p>
<p>In DNP analysis, UV XL-MS is much less well-established, owing to the reduced UV reactivity of double-stranded DNA (dsDNA) compared to RNA [<a href="#B10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10">10</a>, <a href="#B16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16">16</a>]. Alternatively, DNP XL-MS studies use chemicals that react with purines in the DNA [<a href="#B17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17">17–22</a>]. Chemical crosslinking has also been applied successfully in several biochemical studies of RNA–protein interactions [<a href="#B23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23">23</a>], but barely combined with larger XL-MS studies although the potential of alkylating reagents for protein–RNA and protein–DNA crosslinking has been systematically investigated by Scalabrin <em>et al.</em> in 2018 [<a href="#B24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24">24</a>].</p>
<p>To increase coverage of interaction sites we set out to complement UV XL-MS by introducing chemical crosslinking with alkylating agents [mechlorethamine/nitrogen mustard (NM), 1,2:3,4-diepoxybutane (DEB), and formaldehyde (FA)], identifying the crosslinking sites, and to compare the results with those from UV XL-MS. We developed NuXL, a specialized, open-source crosslink search tool, with integrated false discovery rate (FDR) control at the crosslink-spectrum match (CSM) level, and extensive reporting of crosslink results tailored for the analysis of protein–nucleic acid complexes. We demonstrate the feasibility of using XL-MS with NuXL for the integrative modelling of RNPs <em>in vitro</em>, based on the combination of UV and chemical crosslink sites with high-resolution structural data from cryo-EM and crystallography. Moreover, using NuXL we could identify &gt;1500 proteins with their chemically or UV-crosslinked amino acids to RNA, DNA, or single nucleotides in <em>Escherichia coli</em>. The chemically and UV-induced crosslinking sites identified on proteins confirm that chemical crosslinking complements and extends UV crosslinking at the amino acid and nucleotide levels.</p></section><section id="SEC2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials and methods</h2>
<section id="SEC2-1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Crosslinker safety measures</h3>
<p>The chemical crosslinkers used in this study (mechlorethamine/NM, DEB, and FA) are toxic compounds that must always be handled in accordance with the relevant safety regulations. For this study, we kept purchase and use to a minimum.</p></section><section id="SEC2-2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">UV, DEB, NM, and FA <em>E. coli</em> ribosome crosslinking and sample processing</h3>
<p>70S <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes prepared by sucrose-gradient centrifugation as described elsewhere [<a href="#B25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25">25</a>] were kindly provided by Prof. Dr Marina Rodnina (Department of Physical Biochemistry, Max Planck Institute for Multidisciplinary Sciences, Göttingen, Germany) and were stored in 20 mM HEPES, pH 7.5, 100 mM KCl, 7 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, at –80°C. The protein content of the preparation was 13.3 g/l; 50 μg (3.8 μl) of ribosomes were diluted to 100 μl with the same buffer before crosslinking. For UV XL, samples were pipetted into 96-well polypropylene solid bottom microplates (Greiner Bio-One, 655209) and irradiated for 10 min at a 2 cm distance from four parallel UVC Germicidal T5 lamps (Sylvania; G8W; 0000501; λ = 254 nm) as part of an in-house built crosslinking apparatus. DEB crosslinking was performed at a final concentration of 50 mM DEB (Sigma; 202533) for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm. NM crosslinking was performed at a final concentration of 10 mM NM (Sigma; 122564) for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm. FA crosslinking was performed at 1% (v/v) FA (Thermo Scientific™, 28906) for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm. Optimal concentrations of chemical crosslinkers, incubation time and UV irradiation time in order to yield a maximum of CSMs in XL-MS analysis of isolated and crosslinked ribosomes was evaluated in independent experiments (data not shown). All chemical crosslinking reactions were quenched with 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, and incubation for 5 min, room temperature (RT), 300 rpm. After crosslinking and quenching, samples were EtOH-precipitated [75% (v/v) EtOH, 0.3 M NaOAc] and pellets were washed once with 80% (v/v) EtOH. Pellets were dissolved in 45 μl 8 M urea in 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5. Urea concentration was diluted to 1 M using 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5. 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 250 U Pierce™ Universal nuclease for Cell Lysis (Thermo Scientific™, 88700), 100 U nuclease P1 (NEB, M0660S) were added, following incubation for 3 h, 37°C, 300 rpm. Trypsin was added at a 1:20 enzyme-to-ribosome ratio (2.5 μg; Promega, V5111) following incubation ON, 37°C, 300 rpm. Samples were acidified with 2–5 μl 10% (v/v) formic acid to pH 5. 100 U nuclease P1 were added, following incubation for 30 min, 37°C, 300 rpm; 10% (v/v) formic acid and 100% acetonitrile (ACN) were added to reach a final pH of 2 and ACN concentration of 2%. Nucleotide removal was performed using C18 stage tips (Harvard Apparatus; 74-4107) according to manufacturer’s instructions. Briefly, columns were equilibrated with each 400 μl of 100% ACN; 50% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid; and three times 0.1% (v/v) formic acid. Samples were loaded twice and washed two times with 0.1% (v/v) formic acid and once with 5% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid. Elution was performed using twice 150 μl of 50% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid and once 100 μl of 80% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid. All centrifugation steps were performed for 2 min at 600 ×<em>g</em>. Peptides were dried in a speed vac concentrator. Enrichment of crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotides was performed as described in [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>] using Titansphere TiO<sub>2</sub> 5 μm beads (GL Sciences; 5020-75000). Buffer A contained 5% (v/v) glycerol in buffer B; buffer B contained 80% (v/v) ACN, 5% (v/v) trifluoroacetic acid (TFA); buffer B2 contained 60% (v/v) ACN and 0.1% (v/v) TFA; buffer C contained 0.3 M NH<sub>4</sub>OH, pH 10.5. Columns were self-packed by adding ∼3 mg TiO<sub>2</sub> beads in 100% MeOH to p10 pipette tips equipped with a coffee filter insert at the tip (described in more detail by Qamar <em>et al.</em> [<a href="#B26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26">26</a>]). For each replicate, two columns were used. Columns were equilibrated with 60 μl of each 100% MeOH, buffer B and buffer A. Dried samples were dissolved in 60 μl buffer A and loaded twice and washed three times with buffer A, three times with buffer B and once with buffer B2. Elution was performed in three steps of each adding 40 μl buffer C. Elution tubes contained 20 μl of 10% (v/v) FA for pH neutralization. All centrifugation steps were performed for 2–5 min at 900 ×<em>g</em>, to prevent columns from running dry. Eluted crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotides were dried in a speed vac concentrator. Once dry, the samples were dissolved first in 10% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid, and diluted to 2% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid and subjected to liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) measurements.</p></section><section id="SEC2-3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Purification of recombinant Hsh49 and Cus1 from <em>Saccharomyces cerevisiae</em>
</h3>
<p>Recombinant protein complex Cus1-HSH49 was co-expressed from the pETDuet vector in Rosetta II in 2× YT medium ON at 18°C. Purification was performed as described with some modifications [<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">27</a>]. Cells were lysed in 50 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.5, 400 mM NaCl, 500 mM urea, 15% glycerol, 5 mM 2-mercaptoethanol, 20 mM imidazole supplemented with protease inhibitors ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) free (Roche) using sonication. 5kU Pierce™ Universal nuclease for Cell Lysis (Thermo Scientific™, 88700) was added, following incubation for 15 min on ice. Cell debris was removed by centrifugation 17 000 rpm at 4°C for 1 h (A27 rotor; Thermo Scientific™). The supernatant was loaded on a 5 ml His Trap HP Ni-NTA column (Cytiva) equilibrated with lysis buffer A1. Proteins were eluted with a linear imidazole gradient of 20–400 mM in buffer B1 (50 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.5, 200 mM NaCl, 400 mM imidazole, 500 mM urea, 10% glycerol, 5 mM 2-mercaptoethanol) and the main peak fractions containing both proteins were collected and further processed. His-tag was removed by on-column tobacco etch virus protease-digestion (TEV-digestion) and sample was loaded on the pre-packed His Trap HP Ni-NTA column (5 ml; Cytiva). The flow-through was collected and loaded on a pre-packed Heparin HP column (3 × 5 ml, Cytiva). The protein complex was eluted with a linear gradient of 0%–100% of 20 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.5, 1 M NaCl, 10% glycerol, 5 mM 2-mercaptoethanol. The peak fractions containing purified proteins were pooled and concentrated.</p>
<p>A fragment of <em>S. cerevisiae</em> gene encoding nucleotides 1–47 of the U2 small nuclear RNA (snRNA) (ggACGAATCTCTTTGCCTTTTGGCTTAGATCAAGTGTAGTATCTGTTCTtctag) was placed under T7 promoter and cloned into EcoRI/XbaI sites of the pUC57 vector. The plasmid was linearized with XbaI and the U2-47Nt snRNA was transcribed <em>in vitro</em> with T7 polymerase (NEB). After transcription, DNA was digested with RQ1 DNAse and RNA was purified by Microspin G50 spin columns (Cytiva), and precipitated with ethanol.</p>
<p>Hsh49–Cus1–RNA complexes were reconstituted on ice for 30 min at a protein:RNA molar ratio of 4:1.</p></section><section id="SEC2-4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Expression and purification of <em>Saccharomyces pombe</em> Dnmt2</h3>
<p>
<em>Saccharomyces pombe</em> Dnmt2 (spDnmt2) was produced as described before [<a href="#B28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28">28</a>] with several modifications. The protein was expressed from the pGEX 6P-3 vector as a GST-spDnmt2 fusion protein in <em>E. coli</em> BL21(DE3) cells using autoinduction medium (ZYM-5052 medium, 2 mM MgSO<sub>4</sub> replaced by 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, supplemented with 100 μM ZnCl<sub>2</sub>) [<a href="#B28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28">28</a>]. Cells were grown at 37°C for 3 h followed by 60–66 h at 16°C. Cells were disrupted by microfluidization (EmulsiFlex-C3, Avestin) in 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 150 mM NaCl, 1-M LiCl, 2 mM dithiothreitol (DTT) and the crude lysate was cleared by ultracentrifugation at 50 000 ×<em>g</em> for 30 min at 4°C. The cleared supernatant was loaded onto a GSTrap FF column (Cytiva) in 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 150 mM NaCl, 2 mM DTT, and GST-spDnmt2 was eluted with 30 mM reduced glutathione in the same buffer. The eluted protein was incubated with PreScission Protease (1:80 w/w) to cleave off the GST-tag while dialysing against 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 100 mM NaCl, 2 mM DTT for 20 h at 4°C (6–8 kDa MWCO, SpectraPor 1 dialysis membrane) and buffer was exchanged three times. GST was removed via a HiTrap Heparin HP column (Cytiva) in 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 100 mM NaCl, 2 mM DTT using a gradient of 0.1–2 M NaCl. The protein was concentrated using an Amicon ultrafiltration device (10 kDa MWCO, Merck) and further purified via a HiLoad Superdex 75 pg in 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 150 mM NaCl, 2 mM DTT. Purified spDnmt2 was concentrated to 10 mg/ml and stored at −80°C until further use. All chromatographic steps were performed at 4°C. Purified spDnmt2 was dialysed against 50 mM HEPES, pH 7.5, 150 mM NaCl, 2 mM DTT for 16 h at 4°C (6–8 kDa MWCO, SpectraPor 1 dialysis membrane) before complex formation with <em>S. pombe</em>transfer RNA–Asp (sptRNA–Asp).</p></section><section id="SEC2-5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>In vitro</em> transcription and purification of sptRNA–Asp</h3>
<p>Synthetic DNA oligonucleotides were used as a template for run-off <em>in vitro</em> transcription (IVT) of sptRNA–Asp (Fwd: 5′-TAATACGACTCACTATAGGGTCTCCTTTAGTATAGGGGTAGTACACAAGCCTGTCACGCTTGCAGCCCGGGTTCGAATCCCGGAGGGAGAG-3′; Rev: 5′-CTCTCCCTCCGGGATTCGAACCCGGGCTGCAAGCGTGACAGGCTTGTGTACTACCCCTATACTAAAGGAGACCCTATAGTGAGTCGTATTA-3′). The complementary DNA oligonucleotides were annealed at 100 μM for 5 min at 95°C followed by snap cooling on ice. The annealed template was used in IVT reactions containing 1 × Transcription Buffer (Thermo Scientific), 30 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 5 mM DTT, 0.002 U/μl pyrophosphatase (M0361, NEB), 6 mM rNTPs each, 40 ng/μl annealed template, 5 U/μl T7 RNA Polymerase (Thermo Scientific). Reactions were performed at 37°C for 16 h followed by incubation with 0.1 U/μl TURBO DNase (Invitrogen) at 37°C for 30 min. IVTs were subjected to ethanol precipitation in anhydrous alcohol with 300 mM NaOAc, pH 5.2, at −20°C for 16 h. The RNA was pelleted by centrifugation at 20 000 × <em>g</em> for 30 min at 4°C, washed with 70% ethanol, air-dried, and resuspended in 20 mM HEPES, pH 7.5, 50 mM KCl. IVTs were loaded onto a ResourceQ column (Cytiva), washed with 20% buffer B (20 mM HEPES, pH 7.5, 2 M KCl), and eluted in a gradient of 20%–35% buffer B. Eluted fractions were precipitated in anhydrous alcohol with 300 mM NaOAc, pH 5.2, at −20°C for 16 h. The RNA was pelleted by centrifugation at 20 000 × <em>g</em> for 30 min at 4°C, washed with 70% ethanol, air-dried, and resuspended in nuclease-free water. Fractions were analysed in denaturing (7 M urea) 10% polyacrylamide gels and pooled for complex formation with spDnmt2.</p></section><section id="SEC2-6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Purification of human negative elongation factor complex</h3>
<p>The human negative elongation factor (NELF) complex and NELF ΔA, ΔE, and ΔAE tentacle mutants were purified as previously described [<a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">29</a>]. An RNA corresponding to the HIV-1 stem loop (TAR) was purchased from Integrated DNA technologies with the following sequence 5′-CCAGAUCUGAGCCUGGGAGCUCUCUGG-3′. The RNA was folded by incubating 80 μM of the RNA in a buffer containing 100 mM NaCl, 20 mM HEPES/NaOH pH 7.4, 3 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub> at 95°C for 3 min, and transferring to ice for 10 min. NELF (∼12 μM final) and RNA (∼80 μM final) were mixed and incubated for 30 min at 4°C. A complex of NELF + TAR RNA was separated from free RNA by size exclusion chromatography using a Superose6 Increase 3.2/300 column (Cytiva) in a final buffer containing 20 mM HEPES/NaOH pH 7.4, 150 mM NaCl, 10% glycerol, 1 mM DTT. Peak fractions were analysed by polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis and fractions containing both NELF and TAR RNA pooled for further analysis. Fluorescence anisotropy was performed as previously described [<a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">29</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">UV and chemical crosslinking of Hsh49–Cus1–U2 snRNA, Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>, and NELF–HIV TAR RNA <em>in vitro</em> reconstituted complexes and peptide–RNA (oligo)nucleotide enrichment</h3>
<p>Samples containing each a maximum of 50 μg reconstituted Hsh49–Cus1–U2 snRNA, Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>, or NELF:TAR complex were crosslinked and processed as described above for <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes with modifications in crosslinking and digestion conditions. Complex concentrations for the crosslinking reaction were ∼2 mM (Hsh49–Cus1–snRNA), 13.1 μM (Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>), and 1.6 μM (NELF:TAR), respectively. For Hsh49–Cus1–U2 snRNA UV crosslinking, samples were irradiated for 10 min as described above, following EtOH precipitation and washing as described for isolated ribosomes. For DEB and NM crosslinking, 1, 5, 10, and 50 mM DEB or 1, 5, and 10 mM NM were added, following incubation at 37°C for 10 min and precipitation as described for UV crosslinked samples. Samples were dissolved in 4 M urea, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, and diluted to reach 1 M urea, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5. 2 μg of each Ambion™ RNase A (Invitrogen™, AM2270) and RNase T1 (Invitrogen™, AM2283) were added, following incubation at 37°C for 2 h. Afterwards, 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, and 750 U of Benzonase<sup>®</sup> Nuclease (Millipore, 70664) were added, following incubation at 37°C, for another 2 h. Trypsin digestion, nucleotide removal using C18 stage tips, and crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide enrichment were performed as described above for <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. For Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> UV-crosslinking, samples were irradiated for 10 min with consecutive ethanol-precipitation as described for isolated ribosomes. DEB crosslinking was performed using 1, 5, 10, and 50 mM DEB and NM crosslinking of Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> was performed using 1 and 10 mM NM following incubation for 10 min at 37°C. DEB- and NM-crosslinked Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> complexes were ethanol precipitated as described above and after ON incubation at −20°C washed with 80% ice-cold ethanol. Subsequently, samples were digested in 1 M urea, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, using 1 μg of each Ambion™ RNase A, 1 μg of RNase T1, and 250 U Benzonase<sup>®</sup> Nuclease HC (Millipore, 71205) for 2 h at 37°C (benzonase was not applied for digestion of RNA in Hsh49–Cus1–U2 snRNA complex). Trypsin or chymotrypsin (Promega, V1061) were added at a 1:20 enzyme-to-protein ratio following incubation ON at 37°C, 300 rpm. The rest of the protocol is identical to what is described for purified <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. The reconstituted NELF-TAR complex was crosslinked by UV irradiation for 10 min or by adding 1, 5, 10, and 50 mM DEB, or 1, 5, and 10 mM NM and the TAR-RNA element was digested as described for Hsh49–Cus1 complex. All crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide samples were dissolved in 2% (v/v) ACN 0.05% (v/v) TFA for LC-MS/MS measurements.</p></section><section id="SEC2-8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">UV and chemical crosslinking of reconstituted <em>S. cerevisiae</em> mononucleosomes</h3>
<p>Mononucleosomes were reconstituted using the 601 DNA sequence [<a href="#B30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30">30</a>] and purified recombinant <em>S. cerevisiae</em> histones octamers. Reconstitution and histone octamers purification was done as described previously with minor modifications [<a href="#B31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31">31</a>]. In brief, DNA and histone octamers were mixed in a 1:1:1 ratio in a high salt buffer (10 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.6, 2 M NaCl, 0.5 mM EDTA), transferred to Slide-A-lyzer MINI Dialysis devices (Thermo Scientific) and dialyzed via salt gradient dialysis over 16 h into a low salt buffer (10 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.6, 2 M NaCl, 0.5 mM EDTA). After salt gradient dialysis, the sample was centrifuged at 10 000 × <em>g</em> for 1 min to remove precipitates. The DNA concentration was measured using a spectrophotometer, and nucleosome formation was confirmed by running ∼100 ng of nucleosomes on a 3%–12% NativePAGE (Invitrogen). DNA was visualized by SYBR Gold staining.</p>
<p>UV crosslinking of 30 μg mononucleosomes was performed as described above for <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes with minor changes. DEB, NM, and FA crosslinking were performed using 50 mM DEB (we note, that in this case only 50 mM DEB yield crosslinks, while lower concentrations gave no results), or 1 mM NM, or 2% FA, respectively, and incubation for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm. For quenching, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, was added following incubation for 5 min, RT, 300 rpm. Crosslinked protein–nucleic acid conjugates were EtOH precipitated. DNA was digested in 1 M urea, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, with 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 500 U Pierce™ Universal nuclease for Cell Lysis (Thermo Scientific™, 88700), 200 U nuclease P1 (NEB, M0660S), and incubation for 3 h, 37°C, 300 rpm. Protein digest, nucleotide removal and TiO<sub>2</sub> based peptide-DNA-(oligo)nucleotide enrichment was performed as described above for <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. Two TiO<sub>2</sub> columns were used per replicate. Peptides were dried and dissolved like <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes for LC-MS/MS measurements.</p></section><section id="SEC2-9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>In vivo</em> UV and chemical crosslinking of <em>E. coli</em> cells</h3>
<p>
<em>Escherichia coli</em> BL21 DE3 cells were grown in Lysogeny Broth (LB) medium to OD<sub>600</sub> ≈0.6. 15 ml of cell culture (corresponding to roughly 7 × 10<sup>9</sup> cells) were used as starting material per biological replicate for UV, DEB, and NM crosslinking. UV, DEB, and NM crosslinking were performed in medium. Cells were UV crosslinked for 10 min as described above in glass petri dishes (d = 12 cm, vol. cell suspension ≈11 ml, ht. cell suspension ≈1 mm) on an ice-cold metal block for two replicates or at RT for one replicate. DEB crosslinking was performed using 50 mM DEB for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm in 15 ml cell culture. NM crosslinking was performed with 10 mM NM for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm in 15 ml cell culture. DEB and NM crosslinking reactions were quenched with final 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, and incubation for 5 min, RT, 300 rpm. FA crosslinking was performed at 1% (v/v) FA for 10 min, 37°C, 300 rpm in 15 ml cell culture, followed by quenching using 400 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, for 10 min, RT, 300 rpm. Cells were pelleted at 3000 <em>× g</em> for 10 min and the medium was removed. For UV-, DEB-, and NM-crosslinked cells, S30/S100 fractionation by ultracentrifugation was performed as follows: Cell pellets were dissolved in 100 μl of 20 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.6, 100 mM NH<sub>4</sub>Cl, 10.5 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 3 mM β-mercaptoethanol, 0.4 mg/ml lysozyme, 10 U/ml RQ1 DNase (Promega, M6101) and cOmplete™ EDTA-free protease inhibitor cocktail (Roche, COEDTAF-RO). Cells were first incubated at 37°C for 30 min and subsequently mechanically disrupted by sonication in a water bath for 15 min, in alternating 30 s ‘ON’ and 30 s ‘OFF’ cycles at the highest intensity setting (Bioruptor, Diagenode). Three volumes of B-PER™ bacterial protein extraction reagent (Thermo Scientific™, 78243) were added and samples were briefly mixed by inverting, following incubation at RT for 5 min and another mixing by inverting. After another 5 min incubation at RT, samples were centrifuged at 40 000×<em>g</em> for 30 min for S30 fractions, and for another 30 min at 330 000 × <em>g</em> for S100 fractions. S30 and S100 fractions were EtOH precipitated as described above, pellets were washed and dissolved in 8 M urea, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5. LysC (Promega, V1671) was added at a 1:100 enzyme-to-protein ratio, following incubation at 25°C, ON, 300 rpm. Urea concentration was diluted to 1 M using 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5. Trypsin was added at a 1:50 enzyme-to-protein ratio, following incubation ON, 30°C, 300 rpm. Samples were EtOH precipitated as described above and dissolved in 4 M urea, 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5. Urea concentration was diluted to 1 M using 50 mM Tris–HCl, pH 7.5, and 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 500-625 U Pierce™ Universal nuclease, 200–300 U nuclease P1 were added, following incubation for 3 h, 37°C, 300 rpm. Trypsin was added at a 1:50 enzyme-to-protein ratio, following incubation ON, 37°C, 300 rpm. Samples were acidified with formic acid to pH 3–4 and 2% (v/v) ACN was added. Sample clean-up was performed using C18 stage tips as described above. Enrichment of crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotides was performed using 6–8 TiO<sub>2</sub> columns as described above. Eluted crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotides were dried in a speed vac and fractionated by high pH reversed-phase fractionation (see below).</p>
<p>FA crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> cell pellets were resuspended in 300 μl PBS containing 9 mg of lysozyme, following incubation for 5 min, RT; 8 M urea, 100 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.9, 20 mM EDTA was added, and cells were mechanically lysed by sonication, in alternating 30 s on- and 30 s off-cycles at highest intensity output (Bioruptor, Diagenode). Samples were diluted to 1 M urea and trypsin was added at an estimated 1:20 enzyme-to-protein ratio, following incubation at 37°C, ON, 300 rpm. Five mililitres of cell lysate was used for silica enrichment using RNeasy Maxi Kit (Qiagen, 75162) according to manufacturer’s instructions. Each centrifugation step was performed at 3000 <em>× g</em>. Briefly, 3.8 volumes of RLT Buffer, supplemented with β-mercaptoethanol and 2.8 volumes of EtOH were added to the cell lysate. Samples were loaded and washed once with RW1 Buffer, and twice with RPE Buffer. Peptides were eluted twice with H<sub>2</sub>O, and 1 M urea, 12.5 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.9, were added. Trypsin was added at an estimated 1:20 enzyme-to-protein ratio, following incubation for 2 h at 37°C, 300 rpm, following another round of RNA purification using RNeasy Maxi Kit as described above. Samples were adjusted to 1 M urea, 12.5 mM HEPES–NaOH, pH 7.9. 1 mM MgCl<sub>2</sub>, 1 mM ZnCl<sub>2</sub>, 1250 U Pierce™ Universal nuclease, 50 U RNase I, 300 U nuclease P1, 2 U RNase T1, and 200 U RNase A were added, following incubation at 37°C, ON, 300 rpm. Sample clean-up was performed using C18 stage tips as described above. The mixture containing (crosslinked) peptides and RNA oligonucleotides was resuspended in 50 mM NaOAc and 80 U nuclease P1 and 4 U Antarctic phosphatase were added, following incubation at 30°C, 2 h, 300 rpm. Sample clean-up was performed using self-made C18 columns [p200 tips containing three layers of Empore™ C18 SPE Disks (Supelco, 66883-U)]. Eluted FA crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotides were dried in a speed vac concentrator, dissolved in 2% (v/v) ACN 0.05% (v/v) TFA, and subjected to LC-MS/MS measurements.</p></section><section id="SEC2-10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">High pH reversed phase liquid chromatography sample fractionation</h3>
<p>Enriched UV-, DEB-, NM-crosslinked, or whole proteome peptides from <em>E. coli</em> were dissolved in 35 μl 10 mM NH<sub>4</sub>OH, pH 10, 5% (v/v) ACN. Peptides were loaded onto an Xbridge C18 column (Waters, 186003128) using an Agilent 1100 series chromatography system. A flow rate of 60 μl/min was set up using a buffer system consisting of 10 mM NH<sub>4</sub>OH pH 10 (solvent A) and 10 mM NH<sub>4</sub>OH pH10, 80% (v/v) ACN (solvent B). The column was equilibrated with 5% solvent B and developed over 64 min using the following gradient: 5% solvent B (0–7 min), 8%–30% solvent B (8–42 min), 30%–50% solvent B (43–50 min), 90%–95% solvent B (51–56 min), 5% solvent B (57–64 min). The first 6 min were collected as flow-through fraction, followed by 48 × 1 min fractions, which were reduced to 12 fractions by concatenated pooling for crosslink preparations and to 24 fractions for whole proteome. Fractions were dried in a speed vac concentrator and dissolved like <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes for LC-MS/MS measurements.</p></section><section id="SEC2-11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Proteome of non-crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> cells</h3>
<p>For the reference proteome of <em>E. coli</em> cells, cells were treated as described above except that no crosslinking and no TiO<sub>2</sub> purification step was performed. The digested peptides were separated by high pH reverse-phase high performance liquid chromatography (LC) as described above and the resultant fractions were measured by LC-MS as described below.</p></section><section id="SEC2-12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">LC-MS/MS analyses</h3>
<p>All crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide samples were injected onto a C18 PepMap100-trapping column (0.3 × 5 mm, 5 μm, Thermo Scientific™) connected to an in-house packed C18 analytical column (75 μm × 300 mm; Reprosil-Pur 120C18-AQ, 1.9 μm, Dr Maisch GmbH). Columns were equilibrated using 98% solvent A [0.1% (v/v) formic acid], 2% solvent B [80% (v/v) ACN, 0.08% (v/v) formic acid]. Liquid chromatography (LC) was performed using an UltiMate-3000 RSLC nanosystem (Thermo Scientific™). Purified, crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> ribosome samples, yeast nucleosome samples, and high pH reversed phase LC fractions derived from <em>E. coli</em> cell UV-, DEB-, and NM-crosslinked samples were analysed for 58 min using a linear gradient {10%–45% solvent B [80% (v/v) ACN, 0.1% (v/v) formic acid] in 44 min}. Hsh49–Cus1–snRNA and NELF complex samples were analysed for 58 min using a linear gradient from 12% to 50% solvent B in 43 min. Dnmt1–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> samples were analysed for 58 min using a linear gradient from 12% to 50% solvent B in 43 min or from 2% to 48% solvent B in 44 min. FA XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> samples were analysed for 118 min using a linear gradient from 8% to 44% solvent B in 104 min. All measurements ended with an ∼5 min high-percentage solvent B washing step. Eluting peptides were analysed on an Orbitrap Exploris 480 (Thermo Scientific™; <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes, UV, DEB, and NM XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> cells, yeast nucleosomes, <em>E. coli</em> proteome, Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>, NELF complex, Hsh49–Cus1–snRNA), Q-Exactive HF-X (Thermo Scientific™; UV XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> cells), or Orbitrap Fusion (Thermo Scientific™; FA XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> cells) instrument. The following MS settings were used for <em>E. coli</em> ribosome, UV-, DEB-, and NM-crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> cell, yeast nucleosome, Hsh49–Cus1–snRNA, NELF complex, Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> samples: MS1 scan range, 350–1600 <em>m/z</em>; MS1 resolution, 120 000 full width at half maximum (FWHM); AGC target MS1, 1E6; maximum injection time MS1, 60 ms; intensity threshold, 1E4; isolation window, 1.6 Th; normalized collision energy, 30% (DEB, NM, FA) or 28% (UV); charge states, 2+ to 6+; dynamic exclusion, 9 s; top 20 most abundant precursors were selected for fragmentation; MS2 resolution, 30 000, AGC target MS2, 1E5; maximum injection time MS2, 120 ms. For FA XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> samples, the same settings as for <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes were used, except for: MS1 scan range, 350–1580 <em>m/z</em>; maximum injection time MS1, 50 ms; intensity threshold, 2E5; isolation window, 1.4 Th; normalized collision energy, 36%; dynamic exclusion, 20 s; cycle time (top speed method), 3 s; AGC target MS2, 1.1E5; maximum injection time MS2, 80 ms.</p></section><section id="SEC2-13"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">XL-MS data analysis using NuXL</h3>
<p>
<em>Escherichia coli</em> ribosome, Hsh49–Cus1, Dnmt2, and NELF <em>in vitro</em> complexes, yeast <em>S. cerevisae</em> mononucleosomes<em>, E. coli in vivo</em> MS raw data were analysed with the NuXL node in the OpenMS open-source C++ library (version OpenMS-3.0.0-pre-HEAD-2022–04-27-Win64) or in Proteome Discoverer using the following databases: (i) the reference proteome canonical sequence database for <em>E. coli</em> strain downloaded from the UniProt repository on 10 March 2022 (4400 protein sequences) including MaxQuant contaminants (version 2.0.3.0.) for <em>E. coli</em> ribosome and <em>E. coli in vivo</em> MS data, (ii) Hsh49–Cus1 protein sequences for Hsh49–Cus1 data, (iii) <em>S. pombe</em> Dnmt2 protein sequence plus MaxQuant contaminants (version 2.0.3.0.) for Dnmt2 data, (iv) human NELF subunit protein sequences for NELF data, and (v) a custom subset of 75 proteins extracted from the reference proteome canonical sequence database for <em>S. cerevisiae</em> (UP000002311) from the UniProt repository released on 9 January 2025 plus MaxQuant contaminants (version 2.0.3.0.) for yeast nucleosome data. The subset of 75 <em>S. cerevisiae</em> protein sequences was generated for NuXL search because the proteome analysis of the starting nucleosome preparation identified (in addition to the core histones) these 75 other <em>S. cerevisiae</em> proteins, which could also potentially have been crosslinked. The following NuXL presets were selected: <em>E. coli</em> ribosome, RNA-UV (UCGA), RNA-DEB, RNA-NM, RNA-FA; Hsh49–Cus1, RNA-UV (UCGA), RNA-DEB, RNA-NM; Dnmt2, RNA-UV (UCGA), RNA-DEB, RNA-NM; NELF, RNA-UV (UCGA), RNA-DEB, RNA-NM; yeast nucleosome, DNA-UV, DNA-DEB, DNA-NM, DNA-FA; <em>E. coli in vivo</em>, RNA-UV (UCGA), RNA-DEB, RNA-NM, RNA-FA, DNA-UV, DNA-DEB, DNA-NM, DNA-FA. Other parameters were set to default or as follows: enzyme, Trypsin/P, or Chymotrypsin/P (only for Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> datasets derived from chymotryptic cleavage); max. missed cleavages, 2; min. peptide length, 5; max. peptide length, 30; peptideFDR, 0.01; nucleotide adduct length, 2 (for XL-MS datasets from <em>E. coli</em> cells) or 3 (for all other datasets); dynamic modifications, Oxidation (Met). Crosslink MS2 spectra of the <em>in vitro</em> reconstituted complexes (Hsh49–Cus1, Dnmt2, and NELF data, yeast mononucleosomes) were manually curated using OpenMS TOPPView. Further details on XL-MS data processing in NuXL can be found in the <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Methods section</a>. Exemplary run times of NuXL and effects of simple and extended presets on the numbers of identified crosslinks are provided in Supplementary Results.</p></section><section id="SEC2-14"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Label-free quantitative MS data analysis using MaxQuant</h3>
<p>Proteomics data from <em>E. coli</em> cells were analysed with MaxQuant (version 2.0.3.0) [<a href="#B32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32">32</a>] using the reference proteome canonical sequence database for <em>E. coli</em> K12 strain downloaded from the UniProt repository on 10 March 2022 (4400 protein sequences) including MaxQuant contaminants (version 2.0.3.0.). Search parameters were set to default or as follows: enzyme, Trypsin/P; max. missed cleavages, 2; dynamic modifications, Oxidation (Met), Acetylation (protein N-term); static modification, Carbamidomethylation (Cys).</p></section><section id="SEC2-15"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Downstream data analysis</h3>
<p>Barplots were generated using R Studio (version 2024.04.2 Build 764), violin plots were generated using Python (version 3.10.3). Protein 3D structures were plotted in PyMOL Molecular Graphics System (version 3.1.0) using Python (version 3.10.3) scripts for crosslink mapping and distance calculations. Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1</a> was created with BioRender.com.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/62f692a01205/gkaf727fig1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1102" width="741" alt="Figure 1."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>UV and chemical XL-MS workflow for identification of UV and chemical (oligo)nucleotide peptide crosslinks. (<strong>A</strong>) XL-MS processing and analysis workflow. Purified <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes were crosslinked with UV, DEB, NM, or FA. DNA, RNA, and proteins were digested enzymatically, and free nucleotides were removed by C18 chromatography. Crosslinked peptides were enriched by TiO<sub>2</sub> chromatography and analysed by LC-MS/MS. Spectra were evaluated manually and crosslinker-specific adducts were assembled into presets. MS data was then analysed using NuXL. (<strong>B</strong>) Upper panel, stacked bar plot (normalized to 100%) of relative counts of observed crosslinked nucleobases in unique crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide pairs resulting from UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. CSMs were filtered for 1% FDR. Ambiguous, isobaric C–NH<sub>3</sub>/U–H<sub>2</sub>O crosslinks were counted as C/U. Labels are displayed only for values of 7% and above. Lower panel, 100% stacked bar plot of relative counts of observed crosslinked amino acids in unique crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide pairs resulting from UV, DEB, NM XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. CSMs were filtered for 1% FDR and a localization score ≥1. Labels are displayed only for values of 7% and above. FA data are excluded from the visualization due to low numbers of properly assigned crosslinked amino acids. Absolute CSM counts for each bar are depicted below the plots. (<strong>C</strong>–<strong>H</strong>) Schematic crosslink MS2 spectra for UV-, DEB-, NM-, and FA-crosslinked peptide–RNA(oligo)nucleotides. (Crosslinked) peptide fragment b- and y-type ions are shown in wine red and green, respectively. Unmodified fragment ions cover the peptide sequence. For fully annotated MS2 spectra, see <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S2</a>, and for unprocessed mass spectra as visualized from NuXL outputs by TOPPView spectra viewer [<a href="#B70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70">70</a>], see <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary File S2</a>. (<strong>C</strong>) UV crosslink MS2 spectrum of VAVIKAVR-CMP-NH<sub>3</sub>/UMP-H<sub>2</sub>O. Marker ion (C<em>'</em>–NH<sub>3</sub> or U<em>'</em>–H<sub>2</sub>O), precursor ion, and fragment ions modified with C<em>'</em>–NH<sub>3</sub> or U<em>'</em>–H<sub>2</sub>O, as well as unmodified fragment ions indicate a crosslink located at Lys-5. (<strong>D</strong>) UV crosslink MS2 spectrum of NYITESGK-UMP. Immonium ion of tyrosine (iY) modified with U’, precursor ion, and fragment ions modified with UMP or U<em>'</em>, as well as unmodified fragment ions indicate a crosslink located at Tyr-2. (<strong>E</strong>) DEB crosslink MS2 spectrum of peptide–RNA(oligo)nucleotide FWVESEK-DEB-GMP. Precursor ions, and fragment ions modified with DEB + G<em>'</em> as well as unmodified fragment ions indicate crosslink localization at glutamic acid-6. (<strong>F</strong>) NM crosslink MS2 spectrum of crosslinked peptide–RNA(oligo)nucleotide LQHIDFVR-NM-GMP. Marker ion (NM + G’), immonium ion of histidine (iH) modified with NM + G’, precursor ion, and fragment ions modified with NM + G’, as well as unmodified fragment ions indicate crosslink localization at histidine-3. (<strong>G</strong>) NM crosslink MS2 spectrum of crosslinked peptide–RNA(oligo)nucleotide NLEVMVK-NM-AMP. Immonium ions of methionine modified with NM + G’ and NM + GMP, methionine specific loss (–CH<sub>4</sub>S) on the precursor ion and fragment ion y5, as well as unmodified fragment ions indicate crosslink localization at methionine-5. (<strong>H</strong>) FA crosslink MS2 spectrum of crosslinked peptide–RNA(oligo)nucleotide KVEADCR-FA-AMP. Marker ions (FA plus adenine, FA + A<em>'</em>; A<em>'</em>), and precursor ion modified with FA identify AMP crosslink. Panel (A) of the figure created using BioRender: Moshkovskiy, S. (2025) <a href="https://BioRender.com/sr93hcg" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://BioRender.com/sr93hcg</a>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="SEC2-16"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">spDnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> docking-based model generation</h3>
<p>The previously published assembly of <em>sp</em>Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> was generated as described in Johannsson <em>et al.</em> [<a href="#B33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33">33</a>]. It combined <em>de novo</em> RNA modelling protocol [<a href="#B34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34">34</a>] with molecular <em>in silico</em> docking experiments yielding 3D models of <em>S. pombe</em> tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>–Dnmt2 [<a href="#B34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34">34</a>, <a href="#B35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35">35</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-17"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Generation of spDnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> models with AlphaFold3</h3>
<p>Complexes of <em>sp</em>Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> were predicted by AlphaFold3 (AF3; <a href="https://alphafoldserver.com/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://alphafoldserver.com/</a>) [<a href="#B36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36">36</a>]. To enhance structural diversity in predicted models, 60 individual runs with differing random seeds were executed, yielding 300 predictions.</p></section><section id="SEC2-18"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Verification of <em>sp</em>Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> models using XL-MS data</h3>
<p>All 300 AF3-based predictions, along the previously published assembly (“OLD”), were individually assessed for compatibility with XL-MS data using an adapted protocol [<a href="#B37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37">37</a>]. The crosslinking dataset was filtered to retain only crosslinks with a CSM count of &gt;2 per site. Each crosslink was evaluated for compatibility with every tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>–Dnmt2 model by computing Euclidean distances between the protein residue (known position) and the potentially linked nucleotide(s) (unknown position), selected based on distance-based criteria. Two sets of crosslink occurrences were identified for each model: (i) SINGLE, comprising the shortest-distance crosslink pairs between the known protein residues and the closest nucleotides within the defined threshold; and (ii) ALL, including all potential crosslink occurrences within the threshold. Distance thresholds were set according to the crosslinker type: UV (10 Å), DEB, and NM (15 Å). Distances were measured between the N3 atom (for U or C nucleotides) or N7 atom (for other nucleotides) of tRNA and the Cα atom of the crosslinked protein residue. The number of crosslink occurrences within these thresholds was termed “occurrences_d0” (where d0 represents no additional slack beyond the threshold distance). To account for potential conformational flexibility of both protein and tRNA, that was unpredictable by AF3 or <em>in silico</em> modelling/docking, additional protein residue-nucleotide pairs were identified by incrementally increasing the threshold distance by 2, 3, and 5 Å, designated as d2, d3, and d5, respectively. For all predicted tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>–Dnmt2 complexes, both single (d0, d2, d3, d5) and multiple (ALLd0, ALLd2, ALLd3, ALLd5) crosslink occurrences were determined and subjected to further analysis.</p>
<p>To identify models supporting the highest number of crosslinks, we compiled a dataset integrating both ideal (d0) and distance-slacked (d2, d3, d5) single and multiple crosslink occurrences. A custom Bash/Awk script was developed to systematically rank models based on numerical sorting of multiple specified columns, ensuring an unbiased weighting. The script operates in two key steps. During the first step (termed normalization), the maximum value for each specified column was identified to prevent dominance by a single variable. The second step concentrated on scoring and ranking. Here, each row (tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>–Dnmt2 model) was assigned a score reflecting how many of its column values (d0, d2, d3, d5, ALLd0, ALLd2, ALLd3, ALLd5) match the maxima. Rows were then sorted in descending order, with models exhibiting the highest number of maximal values ranked highest. This approach ensured progressive and independent contributions from all criteria.</p>
<p>Our analysis demonstrated that AF3-based predictions exhibit the highest compatibility with crosslinking data, providing compelling evidence of the unprecedented accuracy achieved by AI-driven AF3 models.</p></section><section id="SEC2-19"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Protein annotation sources</h3>
<p>The protein sequences were retrieved from UniProt on 10 March 2022 [<a href="#B38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38">38</a>] and UniProt identifiers were used to transfer the information from additional sources. Domain annotation was downloaded from InterPro [<a href="#B39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39">39</a>]. In addition to that, single-residue annotation of conserved and functionally important residues was obtained using the InterProScan software version 5.56–89.0 on the above mentioned FASTA protein sequences, with residue annotation mode enabled and minimal feature size set to 1 [<a href="#B40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40">40</a>]. Manual review identified 202 relevant InterPro signatures that were manually grouped into 20 categories, corresponding to the molecule class that the protein feature is interacting with (i.e. ATP, FAD/NAD/NADP). Secondary structure was predicted from protein sequences using the S4PRED [<a href="#B41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41">41</a>] using the default settings. Residue-level features were transferred from the DESCRIBEPROT [<a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">42</a>] and the continuous scores were binarized into feature and nonfeature using the default database thresholds.</p>
<p>Protein abundance in ppm for <em>E. coli</em> K12 strain and integrated abundance across strains were downloaded from the PaxDb [<a href="#B43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43">43</a>]. In addition to the pre-computed reference abundance in parts per million (ppm), the MS1 intensities derived from label-free quantification of the non-crosslinked control <em>E. coli</em> dataset were converted into ppm using the PaxDb web-interface. The annotation of RNA-related proteins was derived based on RBP2GO database of putative RNA-binding proteins [<a href="#B44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44">44</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Sampling and statistical testing of feature enrichment</h3>
<p>Statistical testing was performed to quantify the enrichment of amino-acid composition, secondary structure, and other residue features. The foreground was defined as a set of crosslinked peptide residues, either exactly at the crosslink site or belonging to the whole peptide. On the site level, only those CSMs with the localization threshold ≥1 were kept. The number of peptides in the foreground set corresponds to the number of the CSMs per identified sequence. The background was derived by an <em>in silico</em> tryptic cleavage of the proteome. When comparing the crosslinked residue distributions, one random residue per background peptide was chosen.</p>
<p>Since not all the possible tryptic peptides are observable in the MS experiment and 85% of identified experimental sequences were previously reported in PeptideAtlas [<a href="#B45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45">45</a>] and PaxDb [<a href="#B43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43">43</a>], a mass-spectrometry-informed background was defined as a union of all the experimental sequences and all the tryptic <em>E. coli</em> peptides from PeptideAtlas [<a href="#B45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45">45</a>] and PaxDb [<a href="#B43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43">43</a>], and was used to sample the background distribution, omitting the undetectable peptides. A global background consisting of all the MS-detectable peptides was used for the 3D distance enrichment analysis, whilst for feature count enrichment analysis, samples were drawn with replacement from the background distribution to match the experimental distribution of peptide lengths and missed cleavages. This propensity score-based covariate matching was done via matchRanges [<a href="#B46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46">46</a>] using the “nearest” matching option.</p></section><section id="SEC2-21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Quantification and correction of the protein abundance bias</h3>
<p>In order to assess whether the crosslink detection is favouring more abundant proteins we correlated the available protein abundance metrics (below) to the RBP2GO score and the number of observed CSMs in the crosslinking data. When comparing the abundance distributions of the proteins annotated in RBP2GO, the RNA-related proteins appeared to be on average more abundant that other proteins. From this, we concluded that the differences in intracellular protein abundance could affect the identification of crosslinked spectra and the CSM counts of proteins.</p>
<p>We have modified the sampling scheme to compensate for the abundance bias, by associating every peptide with four metrics of the source protein abundance: PaxDB integrated (ppm) and <em>E. coli</em> K12 reference abundance (ppm), log10-transformed label-free MS1 intensity of our noncrosslinked <em>E. coli</em> proteome control and quantile-normalized ppm values derived from the label-free MS1 intensity of our non-crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> proteome control. A combination of multiple features was chosen in order to obtain a balanced abundance estimate informed by both in-house (own proteome data, see above) and public data and cover the most proteins. The abundance-corrected background resulted in comparable amino acid enrichment values, but the frequency of putative RNA-binding residues was reduced, supporting the hypothesis of RNA-related proteins being naturally high-abundant in <em>E. coli</em>.</p></section><section id="SEC2-22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical testing for residue feature enrichment</h3>
<p>The difference of the distribution mean feature counts per residue between the foreground and background was assessed via a <em>t</em>-test as implemented in R language [<a href="#B47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47">47</a>]. In addition, since most of the protein features are distributed not normally, nonparametric goodness-of-fit tests [<a href="#B48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48">48</a>] are used to validate the statistical significance. Furthermore, since many features are discrete, the discrete adaptations of nonparametric tests are used [<a href="#B49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49">49</a>, <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">50</a>]. The significance threshold for the feature enrichment was set to the adjusted <em>P</em>-value of 0.01 (Benjamini–Hochberg method [<a href="#B51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51">51</a>]) to be surpassed by both the <em>t</em>-test and nonparametric goodness-of-fit test. Sampling was repeated 10 times and the resulting statistics were aggregated by mean. The log<sub>2</sub> fold changes between the foreground and background distribution mean feature counts per residue were formatted into a matrix and plotted via the “pheatmap” R library [<a href="#B52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52">52</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-23"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3D distance analysis</h3>
<p>The protein structures were downloaded from the AlphaFold Protein Structure Database [<a href="#B53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53">53</a>, <a href="#B54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54">54</a>] and 3D Euclidean distances were retrieved between the pairs of Cα atoms using the Xwalk [<a href="#B55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55">55</a>] version 0.6 with maximal distance 100 Å. All the amino acids with min. localization score ≥1 were checked for the presence of annotated protein features in the 10 Å radius. Since some of the features of interest occur infrequently, the background was not sampled from the proteome, but a full set of all annotated residues was used as a background distribution. A combination of a <em>t</em>-test and nonparametric goodness-of-fit-test was applied to test for enrichment, same as for the feature counts enrichment.</p></section><section id="SEC2-24"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Over-representation analysis</h3>
<p>The STRING database was used to define the gene sets for <em>E. coli</em>. All the identified proteins with at least one unique peptide were grouped into sets corresponding to different crosslinking methods each [<a href="#B56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56">56</a>]. The hypergeometric test for term over-representation was done via the “fgsea” R package [<a href="#B57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57">57</a>]. A union set of every protein identified in any of RNA-XL datasets or in the non-crosslinked control dataset was used as an enrichment background. The minimal gene set size was set to 4 and maximal to 1000. After testing and <em>P</em>-value adjustment with the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) method, a set of nonredundant terms was derived [<a href="#B51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51">51</a>]. All the terms that pass the significance threshold of 0.05 p.adj BH were tested to not enrich on the background of each other, using the “fgsea” package implementation.</p></section><section id="SEC2-25"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Gene set over-representation heatmaps</h3>
<p>The over-represented STRING terms were selected for heatmap display as long as they passed the p.adj BH significance threshold of 0.01 except the Domains set, where it was set to 0.05. In addition, a query and background set overlap size of at least five proteins was required. Upon the filtering, the manually selected top representative terms were visualized using the “pheatmap” R library [<a href="#B52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52">52</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-26"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Protein feature and coverage plots</h3>
<p>We have compiled a residue-level annotation of <em>E. coli</em> proteome and visualized the co-localization of protein features and RNA-XL coverage. Tryptic cleavage sites correspond to Lys and Arg residues in the proteins. Protein annotation was converted into per-residue GenomicRanges format using the protein coordinates and used for further plotting [<a href="#B58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58">58</a>].</p>
<p>All the CSMs with the localization score ≥1 and their source peptides were plotted for all RNA-XL datasets except FA, where no localization information is available. The positions of RNA-XL sites and peptide coverage were plotted along the protein sequence together with the domain and residue annotation [<a href="#B58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58">58–63</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-27"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Logo plots</h3>
<p>Crosslink site motifs were derived by centering all CSMs with the localization threshold ≥1 around the XL-sites, compared to the background of all MS-detectable peptides in <em>E. coli</em> and plotted via “Logolas” R package with the default settings [<a href="#B64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64">64</a>].</p></section><section id="SEC2-28"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">HTH motif analysis</h3>
<p>Proteins with HTH-motif entries were taken from the InterPro [<a href="#B39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39">39</a>] domain annotation. For every HTH motif type, the average count of crosslinkable amino acids (C, E, F, H, K, M, Y based on observed frequencies) in the motif was calculated, and the fraction of crosslinked amino acids was computed, together with the CSM counts per amino acid.</p></section></section><section id="SEC3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="SEC3-1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Assessment of MS2 spectra and crosslinked species from UV and chemical XL-MS</h3>
<p>Our first goal was to identify DEB-, NM-, and FA-induced crosslink sites at the amino acid and nucleotide level in MS analysis. To this end, purified <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes were subjected to <em>in vitro</em> UV, DEB, NM, and FA crosslinking, nuclease, and trypsin digestion, followed by C18 clean-up for removal of non-crosslinked (oligo)nucleotides and TiO<sub>2</sub> bead-based peptide–RNA (oligo)nucleotide enrichment followed LC-MS analysis (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1A</a>). We manually annotated MS1 and MS2 data, applying known mass-adduct settings for UV and calculating the mass of hypothetical crosslinker–RNA (oligo)nucleotide adducts of various compositions, based on the detected mass adducts and on previous studies suggesting possible reaction mechanisms (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1A</a>, <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figs S1</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">S2</a>, and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S1</a>) [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>, <a href="#B63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63">63–66</a>]. Individual mass adducts on the precursor and fragment ions and several marker ions (crosslinked immonium, nucleobase, and nucleotide ions) were assembled and guided the development of a fast, reliable FDR-controlled computational analysis tool for identifying crosslinking sites, which we named NuXL.</p>
<p>Using NuXL, we determined the occurrence of predominant crosslink types by comparing CSM counts (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1B</a>). For UV, the majority of peptide–RNA (oligo)nucleotides, in absolute CSM numbers, were crosslinks to Lys, Tyr, and Cys. When normalized by the frequencies of the occurrence of the corresponding amino acids in ribosomal proteins (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S3A</a>), Cys and Tyr were found to be the amino acids most frequently crosslinked, in agreement with earlier studies [<a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">4</a>, <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">65</a>, <a href="#B66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66">66</a>] followed by Lys and Met (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S3A</a>). Other aromatic amino acids, such as Phe and Trp, as well as His (all recognized before as preferred UV crosslinking sites [<a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">4</a>, <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">65</a>]) were barely found in isolated ribosomes. Lys crosslinks have been previously identified by Kramer <em>et al.</em> [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>] and Bae <em>et al.</em> [<a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">4</a>]. Most UV crosslinks through Lys involved uracil or cytosine: either uracil with neutral loss of H<sub>2</sub>O, or cytosine with loss of NH<sub>3</sub> (these products are isobaric; Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1</a>C and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>). Cytosine and uracil crosslinks are only distinguishable from one another when no neutral loss of NH<sub>3</sub> or H<sub>2</sub>O is identified. Significantly fewer CSMs of UV crosslinks matched with peptides crosslinked to adenosine or guanosine (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1</a>B and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>). Crosslinks of uracil to Tyr did not reveal loss of H<sub>2</sub>O (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1</a>D and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>).</p>
<p>Unlike UV XL-MS, CSMs from DEB and NM XL-MS ribosome data identified mainly peptides crosslinked to purines. According to unique CSM numbers, 73% of DEB- and 63% of NM-induced crosslinks involved guanine. Adenine was involved in 17% DEB and 22% NM crosslinks. Only a few of the chemical crosslinks were observed to pyrimidines. Both DEB and NM crosslinks, in absolute CSM numbers, were mainly formed to Lys, Met, Glu, His, and Cys, with preferential crosslinking to Glu or Met (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1</a>B and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>). Normalizing the absolute CSM numbers by the frequencies of the respective amino acids in the ribosomal proteins revealed Cys, Met, and His as preferred residues for NM and DEB crosslinking (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S3A</a>). MS2 spectra of peptides crosslinked with DEB unambiguously revealed Glu as crosslinked amino acid (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1E</a>), which differs notably from the reactivity of mainly aromatic or basic residues in other types of protein–RNA crosslinking. MS2 spectra of His crosslinks show prominent immonium ions of His with mass adducts of the crosslinker remnant and/or crosslinker remnant plus the crosslinked nucleobase/nucleotide of adenine and guanine (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1F</a>). For peptides with Met crosslinked by DEB/NM, we frequently observed specific loss of C<sub>2</sub>H<sub>2</sub>S from Met on precursors and fragment ions (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1G</a>). As a result, the MS2 spectra contained high-intensity marker ions comprising the linker remnant of DEB/NM, the crosslinked nucleobase/nucleotide plus C<sub>2</sub>H<sub>2</sub>S. Furthermore, marker ions of only DEB/NM plus C<sub>2</sub>H<sub>2</sub>S were observed.</p>
<p>In MS2, spectra of FA crosslinks differed fundamentally from those of DEB and NM. FA MS2 spectra revealed specific and highly abundant marker ions (FA plus nucleobase/nucleotide) and comprehensive peptide sequence coverage by unmodified fragment ions (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1H</a>). The absence of crosslinked peptide fragment ions, presumably due to the unstable peptide–FA bond, simplified the MS2 spectra and improved database search efficiency, but prevents assignment of the crosslinked amino acid [<a href="#B67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67">67</a>]. Therefore, FA presets contained only a small number of fragment adducts (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S1</a>). A total of 52% of unique FA crosslinks involved adenine, followed by 25% guanine, and 23% cytosine (Fig. <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1</a>B and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>).</p>
<p>In conclusion, chemical XL-MS using DEB, NM, or FA adds complementary information by crosslinking amino acids mainly to purines, while UV crosslinks amino acids (mainly) to pyrimidines. The incorporation of assembled crosslink mass adducts within dedicated presets in NuXL facilitates rapid, FDR-controlled assignment of crosslinking sites.</p></section><section id="SEC3-2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">NuXL—a nucleic acid–protein XL-MS search engine for automated and sensitive crosslink site identification</h3>
<p>Our novel software tool NuXL identifies crosslinked peptide–RNA/DNA (oligo)nucleotides and crosslinked amino acids rapidly and sensitively (for more details, see Supplementary Methods). NuXL is an open-source software and has been developed using the OpenMS software framework [<a href="#B68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68">68</a>]. NuXL is available as standalone version as part of the upcoming OpenMS version and also available as a free node for the Thermo Scientific Proteome Discoverer (PD) software platform (for online tutorials, see <a href="https://openms.de/applications/nuxl/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://openms.de/applications/nuxl/</a>) [<a href="#B69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69">69</a>]. NuXL generates extensive reports at the CSM, peptide, and protein levels, including output for visualization of fragment annotations in the separate viewer application TOPPView for the standalone version of NuXL and in PD [<a href="#B70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70">70</a>]. NuXL allows the user to choose between crosslinker-specific presets (conventional UV, DEB, NM, FA, 4SU, and 6SG; Fig. <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">2A</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S1</a>) that describe the relation between fragment adducts and crosslinked precursor adducts. In the standalone version of NuXL, these presets can be changed by the user to support novel protocols. The NuXL algorithm processes XL-MS data by MS2 spectra pre-processing, theoretical spectrum generation and spectrum-matching, crosslinked amino acid localization, scoring, and subsequent protein inference (Fig. <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">2B</a>; see <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Methods for</a> extended description). For spectrum matching and scoring, we designed subscores that consider XL-MS-specific spectrum features: precursor ions with crosslinker-specific (oligo)nucleotide mass adducts and their associated neutral losses; precursor adduct and crosslinked nucleotide-dependent MS2 fragment ions with (oligo)nucleotide mass adducts (including losses); and singly charged marker ions (i.e. unmodified or chemically crosslinked nucleobase, nucleoside, nucleotide and immonium ions; Fig. <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">2</a>C and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S3</a>). To increase CSM identification rates, subscores are combined and rescored using the Percolator algorithm [<a href="#B71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71">71</a>]. Lastly, the CSM-FDR is calculated; protein groups are inferred from all peptides. NuXL localizes crosslink sites from the pattern of unmodified and crosslinked fragment ions and from the presence of crosslinked immonium ions. Spectra with peak annotations can be visualized in TOPPView and in PD. Comprehensive tutorials are provided online for both the standalone version and the PD version of NuXL (<a href="https://openms.de/applications/nuxl/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://openms.de/applications/nuxl/</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F2"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/96ca8f90e9ba/gkaf727fig2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="373" width="720" alt="Figure 2."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>NuXL software tool for the analysis of XL-MS data. (<strong>A</strong>) Crosslinking reagents supported by NuXL database search tool. Available RNA-, DNA- and crosslinker-specific default and extended NuXL presets. (<strong>B</strong>) NuXL pipeline. XL-MS data-processing includes MS2 spectra pre-processing and spectrum-matching; peak annotation and localization of crosslinked amino acid; post-scoring using the Percolator algorithm and protein inference; visualization of the annotated spectra and generation of tables of results. (<strong>C</strong>) MS2 spectrum features considered in (sub)score calculation. The main score and subscores used by the Percolator algorithm consider the presence and intensities of MS1 and MS2 precursor ions with crosslinker-specific (oligo)nucleotide mass adducts [X(+CL)] including various oligonucleotide combinations and related neutral losses, precursor adduct-dependent MS2 fragment ions with unmodified or chemically crosslinked (oligo)nucleotide mass adducts [a/b/y-ions + X(+CL)], and singly charged marker ions [nucleobase/nucleoside/nucleotide, X(+CL); immonium ions, iX + X(+CL)].</p></figcaption></figure><p>We evaluated NuXL performance using datasets generated for this study including <em>in vitro</em> and <em>in vivo</em> UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS data (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Tables S2</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">S4</a>–<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">S6</a>). Compared with our previous approach in RNP<sup>xl</sup>, the XL-MS data analysis time with NuXL is decreased by several orders of magnitude (for working times see Supplementary Results); this is achieved by implementing NuXL as a specialized search engine with full control over the internal search process, fragment-ion generation, scoring, optimized spectrum comparison, parallelization, and caching of MS2 spectra generated <em>in silico</em> [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>, <a href="#B72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72">72</a>]. Score combination of XL-specific subscores (for details, see Supplementary Methods) and rescoring using Percolator [<a href="#B71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71">71</a>] is fundamental to NuXL’s sensitivity and improved crosslink identification rates at the CSM level for all protocols (1.2- to 8.1-fold higher identification rates). We compared NuXL to the established MSFragger custom offset workflow (XRNAX MassOffset [<a href="#B8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8">8</a>] plus Percolator rescoring, mass shifts from NuXL presets) using our <em>E. coli</em> crosslink data. Our findings revealed a high degree of overlap in identified crosslinks (mean 81% shared CSMs; 88% shared proteins, across all protocols). NuXL showed a higher yield in CSMs. Averaged among different crosslinkers for <em>E. coli</em> dataset, 104% CSMs were uniquely identified by NuXL versus 19% CSMs uniquely identified by MSFragger; 74% distinct proteins were unique to NuXL versus 12% unique to MSFragger, given that MSFragger output is taken as 100%. When we compared NuXL with MSFragger-Labile search [<a href="#B73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73">73</a>], NuXL achieved comparable or higher gains (28% CSMs unique to NuXL versus 23% CSMs unique to MSFragger; 36% distinct proteins unique to NuXL versus 15% unique to MSFragger). Percentages are relative to the total number of CSMs or proteins reported by the MSFragger protocols. For further details on the search engine comparison, see <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Results and</a>  <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S4</a>.</p></section><section id="SEC3-3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">UV combined with chemical crosslinking provides spatial constraints in structural modelling of protein–RNA complexes</h3>
<p>UV XL-MS resolved at the amino acid level provides high-resolution spatial information on RNA interaction interfaces of proteins, which proved to be valuable for understanding the 3D arrangement of biomolecular complexes [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>, <a href="#B9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9">9–11</a>, <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">65</a>, <a href="#B74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74">74</a>]. Our initial crosslinking experiments with isolated <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes demonstrated that chemical crosslinkers connect different amino acids and nucleotides from those connected by UV crosslinking. We therefore applied UV, DEB, and NM XL-MS and NuXL for the analysis of different protein–RNA complexes reconstituted <em>in vitro</em>, Hsh49/Cus1-U2 snRNA [<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">27</a>], Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> [<a href="#B33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33">33</a>], and NELF complex HIV TAR RNA [<a href="#B75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75">75</a>], highlighting XL MS’s role in structural analysis, particularly for RNPs in which the 3D protein structure, but not the 3D RNA structure, was resolved. FA XL-MS was excluded in this set of experiments because of its limited ability to provide crosslink site information.</p>
<p>We analysed yeast spliceosomal protein Hsh49 in complex with Cus1 and U2 47nt-snRNA [<a href="#B26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26">26</a>]. Hsh49 contains two RNA recognition motifs (RRM), and thus is a suitable example of a protein with a well-studied RNA-binding domain [<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">27</a>, <a href="#B76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76">76</a>]. We UV-irradiated the reconstituted Hsh49/Cus1-U2 RNA complex, or titrated it with chemical crosslinkers at various concentrations, and analysed the XL-MS data. For all crosslinking conditions, we identified crosslink sites with CSM numbers increasing gradually with increasing concentrations of the chemical crosslinker. UV and chemical crosslinking sites are located in both RRM domains of Hsh49 as well as in its regions not resolved, and in Cus1 protein (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3A</a>, <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S5</a>, and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S4</a>). The N-terminal RRM domain (RRM1) and the loop structure between two domains of Hsh49 contained more crosslinked amino acids shared between at least two crosslinkers (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3A</a>), while only DEB crosslinking revealed interaction of the RRM2 with U2 47nt snRNA. This result may be consistent with the observation that the N-terminal RRM of Hsh49 was shown to be more important for U2 snRNA binding than the C-terminal one [<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">27</a>]. The loop region connecting RRM1 and RRM2 of Hsh49 was crosslinked more with chemicals, than with UV (in terms of CSMs). The importance of loop region connecting two RRMs was recently shown by Keil <em>et al.</em> [<a href="#B11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11">11</a>] for another yeast RNA-binding protein, Npl3, where mutation of a crosslinked amino acid in such a loop region had severe impact on the function of the protein and, eventually, on the growth of the yeast cells.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F3"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig3.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/ddc2f2d4c529/gkaf727fig3.jpg" loading="lazy" height="396" width="720" alt="Figure 3."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>UV, DEB, NM XL-MS in integrated structural modelling. (<strong>A</strong>) 3D structure of yeast spliceosomal protein Hsh49 in complex with Cus1 (PDB: 5lsb [<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">27</a>]). Surface representation of electrostatic potentials depicted as a colour gradient of ±5.0 k<sub>B</sub>T/e. Hsh49–Cus1 RNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids (Cα) colour-coded depending on crosslinkers are shown based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR, localization score &gt;0, and CSM counts of crosslinked peptides &gt;2. In lower panels, Hsh49 and Cus1 proteins are shown in grey and blue, respectively. The parts of chains not resolved structurally are shown schematically by dashed line.
