Crosslinking was done at 10 min UV irradiation, 10 mM DEB, and 1 mM NM, respectively. (<strong>B</strong>) 3D model of <em>S. pombe</em> methyltransferase Dnmt2 (PDB: 6fdf [<a href="#B33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33">33</a>]) in complex with tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>. Black cartoon tRNA representation: the published model based on UV XL-MS data. Blue cartoon representation: a new model built using AF3 [<a href="#B36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36">36</a>] based on UV and DEB/NM XL-MS data based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR, localization score ≥0, CSM score &gt;2. RNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids that fit to each model are highlighted as in panel (A), while crosslinked amino acids that do not fit are shown in black. Crosslinking was done at 10 min UV irradiation, 50 mM DEB, and 10 mM NM, respectively. (<strong>C</strong>) 3D structure of human NELF complex (PDB: 8uhg [<a href="#B78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78">78</a>], NELF complex as a part of paused transcription complex Pol II-DSIF-NELF; 2bz2 [<a href="#B111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B111">111</a>], RRM domain of NELF-E) including RNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids color-coded and filtered as in panel (A). Intrinsically disordered regions (tentacles) of NELF-A and NELF-E are indicated by pink and grey dashed lines including relative RNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acid positions. NELF-A and NELF-E are shown in pink and dark-grey, respectively, Both NELF-B and NELF-C/D are shown in light grey. The NELF-A tentacle part with a determined structure is spatially located in correspondence with the whole structure of the NELF complex; in contrast, the spatial position of the RRM domain in the NELF-E tentacle is unknown and shown arbitrarily. Crosslinking was done at 10 min UV irradiation, 50 mM DEB, and 10 mM NM, respectively. (<strong>D</strong>) Binding of WT NELF, NELF ΔA tentacle, NELF ΔE tentacle, and ΔAE tentacle to the TAR stem-loop measured by fluorescence anisotropy.</p></figcaption></figure><p>We aimed for determining how the <em>in vitro</em> crosslinking results of the Hsh49/Cus1-U2 RNA complex correspond to the existing structures of this complex in spliceosomes. However, in the only available 3D structure of yeast spliceosome containing Hsh49–Cus1 complex [<a href="#B77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77">77</a>], the proteins are located in poorly resolved regions with multiple RNA regions not modelled at all. Crosslinking of reconstituted spliceosomal complexes containing Hsh49–Cus1 may further improve understanding of their structural interaction with RNAs in spliceosomes.</p>
<p>We also applied UV, DEB, and NM XL-MS to obtain additional spatial restraints for the support of <em>in silico</em> models of <em>S. pombe</em> methyltransferase Dnmt2 in complex with its cognate tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> [<a href="#B33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33">33</a>]. We titrated the reconstituted complex with various chemical crosslinker concentrations and analysed the XL-MS data (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S6</a>). Recent advances in AI-driven structure prediction, particularly AF3 [<a href="#B36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36">36</a>], have enabled unprecedented accuracy in protein–RNA complexes modeling. Motivated by these improvements, we revisited the previously published atomic model of <em>S. pombe</em> methyltransferase Dnmt2 in complex with its cognate tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> [<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">27</a>]. To explore potential structural diversity, we generated 300 spDnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> complexes using AF3, varying the initial random seed. Remarkably, all predictions converged on a consistent binding mode, with only minor variations in tRNA loop conformations (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S7</a>). This AF3-derived model reveals a markedly different tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>–Dnmt2 interaction compared to the previously proposed model, which was based solely on UV crosslinking data (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3</a>B and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S7</a>). In the revised model, adenine 37 is deeply positioned within the cavity containing the methyl-donating <em>S</em>-adenosylmethionine (SAH), while cytosine 38, the target nucleotide for modification, is placed above the ribose moiety of SAH. The biological relevance of this prediction is further reinforced by the placement of guanine 34 directly above cytosine 38, structurally elucidating the enhanced catalytic efficiency of Dnmt2 in the presence of the hypermodified nucleoside queuosine (Q) at position 34 of tRNA<sup>Asp</sup>. Remarkably, despite the fact that AF3 prediction was generated without spatial constraints or prior definition of the protein binding site and its function, the model exhibits a markedly improved fit to crosslinking data compared to previous model. In details, the AF3-based model provided single occurrences of crosslinks fit to the model (occurrences within threshold distances to the tRNA: d0 = 6, d2 = 10, d3 = 11, d5 = 12; see the ‘Materials and methods’ section for more detail) and multiple (ALLd0 = 6, ALLd2 = 10, ALLd3 = 16, ALLd5 = 20). In the previously published model, there were fewer single occurrences (d0 = 4, d2 = 6, d3 = 8, d5 = 9) and multiple occurrences (ALLd0 = 6, ALLd2 = 6, ALLd3 = 9, ALLd5 = 11). Consequently, chemical crosslinking provides additional spatial constraints that complement UV crosslinking and further enhance experimental MS-based validation of structural models, establishing it as an unprecedented tool for modelling RNP complexes.</p>
<p>Finally, we analysed the NELF complex with its four subunits NELF-A/B/D/E, in complex with HIV TAR RNA [<a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">29</a>]. For this, we titrated the reconstituted complex with the chemical crosslinkers in various concentrations and performed XL-MS analysis (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3C</a>, <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S8</a>, and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S4</a>). We identified numerous crosslink sites in the so-called tentacle regions located at the C-termini of NELF-A and NELF-E, most of which are disordered. The tentacle of NELF-E contains an RRM domain and crosslinks by all types of crosslinker to the RNA (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3C</a>). A recent study modelled the structure of the residues 253–266 in the NELF-A tentacle using cryoEM data [<a href="#B78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78">78</a>] and highlighted its role in interaction with the conservative protrusion of the RNA polymerase II subunit, RPB2. Residues in within this particular NELF-A region are believed to interact with the DNA–RNA duplex in the complex [<a href="#B78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78">78</a>]. Accordingly, three crosslinks generated by UV and DEB are clustered around this region in the NELF-TAR interaction (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3C</a>). In addition, fluorescence anisotropy experiments with mutants of the NELF complex showed significantly reduced binding of TAR to RNA upon deletion of intrinsically disordered regions of either NELF-A or NELF-E, and an even greater loss in binding affinity when both regions were deleted (Fig. <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3D</a>).</p>
<p>With regards to database search space of crosslinks for these and other samples of low complexity (i.e. mononucleosomes, see below) we used mainly a database comprising the proteins within the sample, if preparations of protein expressed <em>in vitro</em> revealed sufficient purity, which can be monitored by proteome analysis of the preparation of the reconstituted protein–RNA/DNA complex). We recommend that in these cases the MS2 spectra, and hence the crosslinks need to be validated by applying additional filters or manual inspection (for example, with TOPPView output or PD output), as relying only on the FDR is not possible in these cases. According to our observations, the search against extended databases, e.g. MaxQuant contaminants, in absence of these contaminants in the samples of <em>in vitro</em> reconstituted complexes, could decrease the sensitivity of the searches, such that crosslinks with a small peptide moiety can be wrongly attributed to irrelevant peptides, despite the high-quality spectra. Overall, our data demonstrate that UV and chemical crosslinking cover different sites within RNPs reconstituted <em>in vitro</em>, and hence are valuable for mapping extended RNA-binding interfaces in proteins and for providing suitable restraints for <em>in silico</em> docking of RNA to proteins.</p></section><section id="SEC3-4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">NuXL facilitates identification of UV and chemical crosslinking sites in <em>E. coli</em>
</h3>
<p>To assess chemical XL-MS and NuXL <em>in vivo</em>, we applied our method to <em>E. coli</em> cells. After crosslinking with UV, DEB, NM, or FA, cells were lysed, enriched for crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotides, and analysed with LC-MS. We note that for FA XL-MS we used a different workflow based on silica enrichment subsequent to TRIZOL extraction of intact (crosslinked) RNA (see the ‘Materials and methods’ and ‘Discussion’ sections). NuXL identified 4924 unique crosslinked peptides across 1502 proteins, with varying numbers of peptides and proteins identified by each XL-MS method (1007, 2715, 2805, and 670 crosslinked peptides, and 393, 952, 1186, and 302 crosslinked proteins, were identified by UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS, respectively) (Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4</a>A and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S5</a>). A total of 162 proteins were found crosslinked under all four crosslinking conditions. In accordance with the results for crosslinking of isolated <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>), UV crosslinked mostly pyrimidines (51% cytosine/uracil, 39% uracil, 2% cytosine), and the chemical crosslinkers most frequently gave purine crosslinks (63%, 70%, 51% guanine, and 32%, 26%, 38% adenine for DEB, NM, and FA, respectively; Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4B</a>). The most frequently identified crosslinked amino acids in unique crosslinks in UV XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> data were Lys (41%), Tyr (14%), and Cys (7%). For DEB XL-MS, we identified Met (42%), His (14%), and Lys (11%); for NM XL-MS, Met (64%), His (9%), and Cys (5%) (Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4B</a>). After normalizing the absolute CSM numbers by the amino acid frequencies of their occurrence in the sequences of all crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> proteins, Cys, His, and all aromatic amino acids were found to be the most frequently UV-crosslinked amino acids, which is consistent with results of previous studies [<a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">4</a>, <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">65</a>] (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S3B</a>). Since we identified crosslinking sites of Phe and Trp in the <em>E. coli</em> XL-MS data, but not in isolated ribosomes (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S2</a>), we conclude that their absence in <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes may be a specific characteristic of ribosomal RNA-binding proteins. This observation can be partially attributed to the reduced frequencies of Phe and Trp in the sequences of 58 <em>E. coli</em> ribosomal proteins when compared to the overall background of the whole proteome (3.1% versus 3.8% for Phe, and 0.6% versus 1.5% for Trp, Fisher’s exact test <em>P</em>-value for both &lt;.01). Crosslinking by UV, DEB, and NM in <em>E. coli</em> dataset at specific positions was independent on the surrounding sequence context as shown in Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4C</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/4ab7d6d42f85/gkaf727fig4.jpg" loading="lazy" height="756" width="751" alt="Figure 4."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>
<em>In vivo</em> UV and chemical XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> cells. (<strong>A</strong>) Venn diagrams of unique crosslinked proteins (left) and peptides (right) for UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> cells. Identifications are filtered for 1% FDR at CSM level. (<strong>B</strong>) Left panel, stacked bar plot (normalized to 100%) of relative counts of observed crosslinked nucleobases in unique crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide pairs resulting from UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. CSMs were filtered for 1% FDR. Ambiguous, isobaric C–NH<sub>3</sub>/U–H<sub>2</sub>O crosslinks were counted as C/U. Labels are displayed only for values of 7% and above. Right panel, 100% stacked bar plot of relative counts of observed crosslinked amino acids in unique crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide pairs resulting from UV, DEB, NM XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. CSMs were filtered for 1% FDR and a localization score ≥1. Labels are displayed only for values of 7% and above. FA data are excluded from the visualization due to low numbers of properly assigned crosslinked amino acids. Absolute CSM counts for each bar are depicted below the plots. (<strong>C</strong>) Logo plots of amino acid positional frequency enrichment around the RNA-XL sites for UV, DEB, and NM. The localization score threshold of ≥1 was used to compare the detected CSM counts per position against the amino acid frequency in the MS-detectable <em>E. coli</em> tryptic peptides. (<strong>D</strong>) Cartoon representation of cryo-EM 3D structure of <em>E. coli</em> 70S ribosome-EF-TU complex (PDB: 5afi [<a href="#B79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79">79</a>]) and histograms of crosslink distances. In the structures, RNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids are highlighted as spheres (Cα atoms) in red (UV), purple (DEB), or blue (NM); or as green cartoon (FA). Crosslink sites are based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR for all conditions, and localization score ≥1 for UV, DEB, and NM. Histograms display distances (in Å) between UV (left), DEB (centre left) and NM (centre right) crosslinked amino acids (Cα) and closest atom of the closest nucleotide in the <em>E. coli</em> 70S ribosome-EF-TU complex structure. (<strong>E</strong>) Cartoon representation of the cryo-electron microscopy structure of a trimer of <em>E. coli</em> polynucleotide phosphorylase (PNPase; grey cartoon) in complex with six copies of <em>E. coli</em> RNA-binding protein Hfq (grey cartoon) and the 3′ external transcribed spacer RNA of leuZ (pink cartoon/surface; PDB: 7ogm [<a href="#B81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81">81</a>]). PNPase KH domain and S1 motif are highlighted in limegreen and cyan, respectively. The Arg-83 residue involved in RNA binding is highlighted by green sticks [<a href="#B80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80">80</a>]. Alpha-carbon atoms (Cα) of RNA-(oligo)nucleotide crosslinked amino acids are highlighted as red (UV), purple (DEB), blue (NM), pink (UV and DEB), yellow (UV and NM), or orange (DEB and NM) spheres. Crosslinks are filtered for a localization score ≥1. Crosslinks mapped on the structure of another member of <em>E. coli</em> degradasome, enolase, are shown in <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S9</a>. (<strong>F</strong>) Cartoon representation of the cryoEM structure of <em>E. coli</em> ribosome with signal recognition particle (SRP) bound; for simplicity, ribosomal proteins are not shown (PDB 5nco [<a href="#B84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84">84</a>]). Color scheme of chemical crosslinks is as in panel (E). Mapped crosslink positions are based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR, and localization score &gt;0.</p></figcaption></figure><p>We mapped <em>in vivo</em> UV, DEB, and NM crosslink sites and FA crosslinked peptides of ribosomal proteins onto the 70S <em>E. coli</em> ribosome structure (Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4D</a>) [<a href="#B79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79">79</a>]. For this, we filtered crosslink sites for localization score ≥1, as judging from manual spectrum validation, this threshold secures reliable amino acid localization. We also calculated distances between Cα atoms of crosslinked amino acids and the closest atom of the closest nucleotide for UV, DEB, and NM. The median distances found for UV, DEB, and NM were 6.8, 8.9, and 8 Å, respectively, which agrees well with calculated spacer lengths and consideration of structural flexibility (we accept maximum distances of 10, 15, and 15 Å for UV, DEB, and NM, respectively) and underscores the specificity of chemical crosslinking for <em>in vivo</em> crosslink analysis (Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4D</a>).</p>
<p>We also located the <em>in vivo</em> XL-MS sites of the polynucleotide phosphorylase (PNPase) on available 3D structures of the protein trimer in complex with six copies of HfQ and RNA (Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4E</a>) [<a href="#B80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80">80</a>, <a href="#B81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81">81</a>]. PNPase UV-crosslinking sites are positioned in the cavity of the PNPase tetramer core, and their locations align with the amino acids responsible for the RNA degradation activity of the PNPase [<a href="#B81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81">81</a>]. Despite the fact there are amino acids, such as lysines in the cavity that are potentially chemically crosslinkable, we did not identify these as crosslinked to RNA. This might indicate that spatial constraints in this cavity/channel, when occupied with RNA, exist that prevent penetration of the chemical crosslinker NM and DEB inside the cavity/channel. Chemical crosslinking sites are located mainly in, or very close to the S1/KH domain of the PNPase that binds to (small) RNAs and guides them towards degradation. PNPase forms the <em>E. coli</em> degradasome with proteins RNaseE, RhlB, and enolase and we also identified crosslinking sites in these proteins (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S5</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary File S1</a>) [<a href="#B82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82">82</a>]. Enolase also plays an essential part in the glycolysis pathway; the crosslink sites are scattered across the enzyme, with no visible tendency to cluster, for example, at positively charged surface patches. This finding seems to contradict the specific binding of nucleotides between the enolase dimer, even though there we found several crosslinks located in the cleft between two enolase monomers in close vicinity to the active site of the enzyme (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S9</a>). In addition, it was shown that, in mammalian cells, enolase can bind hundreds of mRNAs which regulate its enzymatic activity, the effect which could be conserved through different kingdoms of life [<a href="#B83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83">83</a>]. Hence, the different crosslink sites might reflect the various contacts of the different RNAs.</p>
<p>To illustrate the capacity of the chemical crosslinker to link double-stranded RNA to a protein, we mapped chemical crosslinks on a structure of SRP in contact with 23S ribosomal RNA and 4.5S SRP RNA (Fig. <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4F</a>) [<a href="#B84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84">84</a>]. Crosslinked amino acids are in alpha-helices of SRP54 and FtsY proteins in immediate proximity either to 4.5S RNA of SPR or to 23S ribosomal RNA.</p></section><section id="SEC3-5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Properties of crosslinked <em>E. coli</em> proteins</h3>
<p>Because of the large number of detected crosslinking sites, we aimed to assess whether crosslinking is associated (i) with the abundance of crosslinked proteins in the <em>E. coli</em> proteome (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S6</a>), thus possibly reflecting a “crowding effect” (i.e. less specific crosslinking of only highly abundant proteins to nucleic acid in the crowded cellular environment), in particular when chemical crosslinkers are used, and (ii) with the molecular features of the crosslinked proteins. For the latter, we integrated residue properties and 3D structure annotations (see below).</p>
<p>To investigate the abundance of crosslinked proteins detected across XL-MS methods, we applied label-free MS to <em>E. coli</em> cells grown under the same conditions as for our crosslinking experiments and also used abundance values available for <em>E. coli</em> K12 in the PaxDb [<a href="#B43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43">43</a>] and PaxDb_integrated across strains (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S10A</a>, see also the ‘Materials and methods’ section). In general, we observed a moderate 0.42–0.53 Pearson correlation values between the number of CSMs and protein abundance and 0.48–0.54 when comparing the number of CSMs to the RBP2GO score (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S10B</a>). According to STRING [<a href="#B56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56">56</a>] and RBP2GO [<a href="#B44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44">44</a>] annotation, putative RNA-binding proteins are naturally highly abundant in <em>E. coli</em>, while most proteins without RNA-related functions have lower abundance. Nevertheless, there also exists a group of high-abundant proteins without annotated RNA-related functions. Our own proteome analysis is mainly in agreement with this annotation; however, we detected a small subset of abundant non-RNA-binders as well, possibly reflecting molecular crowding. With regard to the crosslinked proteins, we observed that DEB and NM crosslinking covered most annotated RNA-binders, whilst UV and especially FA identified predominantly top-abundant proteins. Crosslinks were mainly identified in high-abundance RNA-binder proteins, but also (upon DEB and NM treatment) in the low-abundant putative RNA-binders and the above-mentioned subset of non-RNA-binder abundant proteins. We compared the iBAQ values of identified proteins in our <em>E. coli</em> proteome study and the number of potentially crosslinkable peptides (which is higher in abundant RNA-binders). DEB and NM crosslinking managed to identify less abundant and crosslink-reactive proteins (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S10C</a>). We observed crosslinking only for a small fraction of other high-abundant proteins and no stochastic crosslinking of lower-abundant proteins without RNA-related functions.</p>
<p>When comparing the difference between the RNA-binding and nonbinding protein abundance (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S10D</a>), we observe that the correlation of abundance and RNA-binding in <em>E. coli</em> proteome is reflected in all datasets, biasing FA the strongest, followed by UV, and with NM and DEB being the least affected by molecular crowding.</p>
<p>We statistically tested the enrichment of amino acid residue features of crosslinked amino acids and peptides against an expected background distribution of <em>E. coli</em> peptides, considering peptide length and tryptic missed cleavages and protein abundance (see Methods for details). The DESCRIBEPROT database protein annotations were transferred for all identified crosslinked peptides (for FA XL-MS) and sites (for UV, DEB, and NM XL-MS) at the residue level. We found that secondary structure elements were only mildly associated with crosslinking events (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5</a>A and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S7</a>). The analysis of residue properties revealed the under-representation of putative disordered residues at the crosslink sites, but an overall enrichment of disordered RNA and DNA binders on the crosslinked peptides (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5A</a>). The strongest enrichment was observed for specific amino acids at crosslinked sites (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S11</a>), confirming the amino acid preferences displayed in Figs <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1B</a> and <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4B</a>. We investigated the correlation of abundance and RNA-binding by sampling the background peptide distribution with regard to the protein abundance, resulting in a more abundant background compared against the detected peptide set. We observed that residue properties, such as solvent exposure and evolutionary conservation of the crosslinked amino acids did not change much, whilst the enrichment of RNA-binding decreased, yet retaining the moderate enrichment upon almost full abundance bias compensation (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S11</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig5.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/aada5f325701/gkaf727fig5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="735" width="720" alt="Figure 5."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Crosslink-assisted functional and structural protein characterization. (<strong>A</strong>) A heatmap of log2 fold changes between the experimental and an <em>in-silico</em> background distribution mean feature counts per residue. The background sampling was performed with regards to the experimentally observed peptide length and missed cleavage distribution and source protein quantity to compensate for the abundance bias. The non-crosslinked control and FA datasets were analysed on the peptide level, whilst the UV, NM, and DEB enrichment was analysed on crosslinked residue level. The NA values correspond to the cases where the sampling or statistical testing could not be performed due to the feature distribution violating the method assumptions. (<strong>B</strong>) A gene set over-representation by the crosslinked protein sets. Reported are the terms significant in at least one crosslinking method (p.adjusted BH ≤ 0.01 for all sets and 0.05 for protein domain sets). The gene set coverage is colour coded, with the number of identified proteins noted in the heatmap cells and the full gene set size next to the corresponding rows (<strong>C</strong>) Cartoon/surface representation and electrostatic surface of AlphaFold-predicted 3D structure of <em>E. coli</em> inosine-5′-monophosphate dehydrogenase (AF-<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/protein/P0ADG7" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">P0ADG7</a>-F1) with RNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids (Cα) highlighted as purple (DEB), blue (NM), orange (DEB and NM), or lime-green (UV, DEB, and NM) spheres. Mapped crosslink positions are based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR, and localization score ≥1. The Bateman domain is highlighted as a transparent blue surface. Conserved residues (derived from conserved domain database CDD) involved in IMP binding are highlighted by a yellow transparent surface. (<strong>D</strong>) Cartoon representation and electrostatic surface of the crystal structure of succinate-CoA ligase subunit alpha (upper panel) with bound coenzyme A (yellow sticks) and subunit beta (lower panel) with bound ADP (yellow sticks) (PDB: 1cqi [<a href="#B112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B112">112</a>]). Crosslinks are mapped as in C with FA-crosslinked peptide displayed as green cartoon and UV-crosslinked amino acids (Cα) highlighted as red spheres. Met-245 crosslink found to a dinucleotide is marked by a green-outlined box.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Taken together, the preferential specificity of RNA-binding protein detection, the lack of crosslinkable residue saturation and the enrichment of RNA-binding residues beyond abundance bias all strongly support the specificity of our (chemical) crosslinking methods (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Figs S3B</a>, <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">S10A</a> and C, and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">S11</a>) and confirm the importance of the observation that RNA-binding proteins are naturally highly abundant in <em>E. coli</em>.</p>
<p>It is important to note that the proteome of <em>E. coli</em> exhibits significant plasticity depending on the growth conditions [<a href="#B85" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B85">85</a>]. In this work, we characterized RNA-binding proteins in nutrient-rich media where cells strongly allocate their resources to translation. This may result in higher concentrations of RNA-binding proteins. Under starving conditions that are closer to natural situations, the distribution of different functional groups of proteins may differ from our observations.</p>
<p>Further, we analysed crosslinked protein sets under different XL-MS conditions for gene ontology (GO) term enrichment using STRING database annotations (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5</a>B and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S8</a>). The most enriched GO terms are related to the cytosol, nucleic acid binding, and translation and those for biosynthetic processes. Regarding the latter, we found almost all proteins involved in glycolysis and the tricarboxylic acid cycle (TCA) and their crosslinked amino acids linked to mono- and dinucleotides. Other studies of the RNA interactome in <em>E. coli</em> and eukaryotes also identified metabolic enzymes crosslinked to RNA, but barely with their crosslinked amino acid (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S12A</a>) [<a href="#B6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6">6</a>, <a href="#B83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83">83</a>, <a href="#B86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B86">86</a>, <a href="#B87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B87">87</a>]. In glycolysis, 27% of enzymes were crosslinked across all XL-MS conditions, and 80% under at least one (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S13A</a>). Over half of these enzymes were linked to dinucleotides or cytosine/uracil, indicating RNA-binding properties. Similar trends were observed in the TCA pathway (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S13B</a>). Our analysis also revealed an overrepresentation of coding and noncoding RNA-related and metabolic terms and domains, including RNA-binding domains, as well as S1 and OB fold domains, and cofactor-binding domains such as the AAA domain (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5B</a>).</p>
<p>To globally determine if (RNA) crosslinking in cofactor-binding metabolic proteins occurs at consistent or varying sites, we mapped UV, DEB, and NM crosslink sites onto the sequences and structures of proteins (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary File S1</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S14</a>). We assessed co-localization of crosslinked residues and functionally important amino acids from DESCRIBEPROT and InterPro databases. We compared the 3D distances between C<sub>α</sub> of the crosslinked amino acids and the C<sub>α</sub> of residues with annotated features against a background distribution (see the ‘Materials and methods’ section for details) and found that RNA-binding residues, disordered regions, conserved residues and molecular recognition features (i.e. protein interactions) were enriched within a 4 Å radius of crosslink sites (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S14A–G</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Tables S7</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">S9</a>). Crosslinked sites were significantly closer to RNA-binding regions and further away from DNA-binding areas (see below). Additionally, crosslink sites were enriched adjacent to coenzyme A and FAD/NAD/NADP binding sites. Crosslink sites across all methods were close to ATP-binding residues (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S14H–L</a>).</p>
<p>To further provide structural insights, we show protein structures with their bound mononucleotides/co-enzyme A in Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5C</a> and <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">D</a>. For inosine 5′-monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMDH; AF-<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/protein/P0ADG7" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">P0ADG7</a>-F1), an enzyme involved in guanosine-nucleotide metabolism, amino acids crosslinked chemically to adenine and guanine are in close proximity to the Bateman domain of the predicted 3D structure (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5C</a>). The Bateman domain is known to be involved in RNA and single-stranded DNA (ssDNA) binding [<a href="#B88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B88">88</a>, <a href="#B89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B89">89</a>]. In addition, several crosslinks close to conserved residues in the active site, which binds IMP, were identified. The crosslink data support the suggestion of regulatory roles for RNA associated with ATP and GTP binding in the Bateman domain [<a href="#B90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B90">90</a>]. We further mapped crosslinks to the structure of succinyl-CoA synthetase subunits alpha and beta (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5D</a>). In SUCD, crosslinks were identified close to bound coenzyme A, including a dinucleotide crosslink of unambiguous RNA origin. In SUCC, crosslinks are rather scattered across the whole structure, without any apparent tendency to cluster at the ADP-binding site. This may provide a hint toward the possible involvement of SUCC in RNA-related processes, which could bring RNA molecules close to the complex and lead to more dispersed crosslinks across this relatively small protein.</p></section><section id="SEC3-6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemical XL-MS identification of protein–DNA crosslinks—DNA-specific NuXL settings</h3>
<p>We adapted our chemical NuXL presets for the analysis of protein–DNA crosslink sites by comparing chemical and UV XL-MS of 601 DNA sequence [<a href="#B30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30">30</a>] bound to yeast octamers as part of an <em>in vitro</em> reconstituted nucleosome (Fig. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S10</a>). Sample processing, including crosslink enrichment, was performed as described above for the analysis of <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. In addition to peptides crosslinked to the nucleobase moiety of DNA, we observed precursor-ion mass adducts of 178.00311 Da (C<sub>5</sub>H<sub>7</sub>O<sub>5</sub>P) or 196.01368 Da (C<sub>5</sub>H<sub>9</sub>O<sub>6</sub>P), matching deoxyribose-phosphate adducts with and without loss of H<sub>2</sub>O, respectively. Most likely, these are derived from abasic sites in the DNA generated by the crosslinker reaction and lead to the generation of a reactive aldehyde that crosslinks to the proteins (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S15A–C</a>) [<a href="#B91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B91">91</a>, <a href="#B92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B92">92</a>]. We did not observe a similar species in protein–RNA crosslinking.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/87d51a090633/gkaf727fig6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="479" width="787" alt="Figure 6."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Protein–DNA UV and chemical XL-MS of <em>S. cerevisiae</em> nucleosomes. (<strong>A</strong>) <em>Saccharomyces cerevisiae</em> nucleosome core-particle structure (PDB: 1id3 [<a href="#B113" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B113">113</a>]) with DNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids (Cα) highlighted as red (UV), purple (DEB), or blue (NM) spheres based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR, localization scores &gt;0, and CSM counts of crosslinked peptides &gt;2. For FA, whole crosslinked peptides filtered for 1% FDR, and CSM counts &gt;2 are shown in green. (<strong>B</strong>) Upper panel, stacked bar plot (normalized to 100%) of relative counts of observed crosslinked nucleobases in unique crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide pairs resulting from UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS of <em>S. cerevisiae</em> nucleosomes. CSMs were filtered for 1% FDR. Labels are displayed only for values of 7% and above. Lower panel, 100% stacked bar plot of relative counts of observed crosslinked amino acids in unique crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide pairs resulting from UV, DEB, NM XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> ribosomes. CSMs were filtered for 1% FDR and a localization score ≥0.5. Labels are displayed only for values of 7% and above. FA data are excluded from the visualization due to low numbers of properly assigned crosslinked amino acids. Absolute CSM counts for each bar are depicted below the plots.</p></figcaption></figure><p>UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS sites were mapped to structures of the <em>S. cerevisiae</em> nucleosome core particle (Fig. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6A</a>). For UV, DEB, and NM, most crosslinked amino acids were found to be clustered in close proximity to the DNA. UV XL-MS mostly identified unique crosslinks to deoxyribose (28%) and cytosine (26%) with lysine as the most frequently crosslinked amino acid (43%; Fig. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6B</a>). DEB XL-MS identifies mostly crosslinks to deoxyribose (48% of unique crosslinks), followed by all bases except cytosine (all 16%) involving mainly lysine (28%) and threonine (21%) as crosslinked amino acids (Fig. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6B</a>). For NM XL-MS, deoxyribose crosslinks were also the most strongly represented (46%), and guanine was a next preferential moiety for crosslinking (25%). Amino acid preferences of NM were similar to those of DEB. In the UV-irradiated sample, in addition to lysine and threonine, histidine and tyrosine were frequently crosslinked in histone sequences. The discrepancy between crosslinked amino acid frequencies in nucleosomes (Fig. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6B</a>) and in RNA crosslinking samples (Figs <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">1B</a> and <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">4B</a>) may be explained by the sequences of the four yeast histones, which contain no cysteines and only one methionine (except for the starting residues). We measured distances between Cα atoms of crosslinked amino acid residues that were resolved in the yeast nucleosome structure (Fig. <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6A</a>) and the closest atom of the closest nucleotide. The median of amino acid–dNTP distances was 8.5, 7.6, and 8.1 Å for UV, DEB, and NM, respectively. No significant difference between crosslinkers was observed in contrast to <em>E. coli</em> RNA crosslinking data, most likely, due to much fewer numbers of nucleosomal crosslinks. As in RNA–protein XL-MS experiments, in DNA–protein FA XL-MS the crosslinked amino acid could barely be identified in most cases. The yield (CSM and unique crosslinked peptides) of FA crosslinking was lower than that of the other crosslinking methods; one unique crosslinked peptide derived from H2A, three from H2B, one from H3, and one from H4. FA XL-MS identified no deoxyribose as crosslinked residue in contrast to the other chemical crosslinkers, which were dominant in NM and DEB, and hence more crosslinked peptides were identified with these chemicals (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S10</a>).</p></section><section id="SEC3-7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Crosslinking of DNA-binding proteins in <em>E. coli</em>
</h3>
<p>We searched our <em>in vivo</em> crosslink <em>E. coli</em> dataset with NuXL and DNA presets (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S11</a>). We identified the cold shock domain containing Csp protein family members crosslinked to RNA and DNA according to chaperone properties in binding single-stranded RNA and DNA [<a href="#B93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93">93</a>, <a href="#B94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B94">94</a>]. The crosslinking sites to RNA and DNA are at the same amino acids in these proteins. A similar pattern is present in the ssDNA binding protein SSB, in which the same residues crosslink to DNA and RNA. We also identified RNA and DNA crosslinks in the nucleoid dsDNA-binding proteins H-NS, DBHA/DBHB, FIS, and IHF (Fig. <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">7A</a>). We mapped IHFA and IHFB crosslinks to the EM 3D structure of the Cas1–Cas2–IHF–DNA holo-complex (Fig. <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">7A</a>). The UV and chemical RNA crosslinking sites are located in close proximity to the dsDNA in the structure. Potential RNA-binding was previously suggested in acid-resistance studies [<a href="#B95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B95">95</a>]. All other nucleoid proteins show RNA and DNA crosslinking at the same positions in the 3D structures, which indicates RNA-binding properties of nucleoid proteins in general (Fig. <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">7A</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12359043_gkaf727fig7.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5000/12359043/09afd575f078/gkaf727fig7.jpg" loading="lazy" height="665" width="787" alt="Figure 7."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Examples of crosslinked DNA-binding proteins in <em>E. coli</em>. (<strong>A</strong>) Cartoon representations of nucleoid proteins H-NS (AF-<a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/protein/P0ACF8" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">P0ACF8</a>-F1), integration host factor (subunit alpha and beta; <em>PDB:</em> 5wfe [<a href="#B114" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B114">114</a>]), HU (alpha and beta; PDB: 4yew [<a href="#B115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B115">115</a>]), and FIS (as homodimer; PDB: 3iv5 [<a href="#B116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116">116</a>]) crosslinked to RNA (and DNA) in <em>E. coli</em>. DNA is displayed as dark cyan cartoon/surface. RNA- and unambiguous DNA-(oligo)nucleotide-crosslinked amino acids (Cα) are highlighted as red (UV), purple (DEB), blue (NM), light-pink (UV and DEB), yellow (UV and NM), orange (DEB and NM), or lime-green (UV, DEB, and NM) spheres. FA-crosslinked peptides are displayed as green cartoon. RNA- and unambiguous DNA-crosslink labels are coloured grey and purple, respectively. Labels indicate crosslinked amino acids and directly crosslinked nucleic acid moiety. Crosslink sites are based on CSMs filtered for 1% FDR for all conditions, and localization score ≥1 for UV, DEB, and NM peptide–RNA(oligonucleotide)-crosslinks. (<strong>B</strong>) Cartoon representation of the crystal structure of topoisomerase I (PDB: 4rul [<a href="#B117" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B117">117</a>]) with crosslinks and labels as in panel (A). In the case of UV-crosslinked peptide spanning amino acids 774–788, cartoon is coloured in red and Cα of putatively crosslinked phenylalanines Phe-780 and Phe-786 are highlighted by spheres. For Cys-401, and Cys-763, the crosslink sites were manually annotated based on the reactivity of cysteine residues. Crosslinks are not filtered based on localization score. Conserved residues of the DNA binding groove (CDD1: cd00186) and the active site Tyr-319 are highlighted by yellow and cyan sticks, respectively. The distances between Cα of crosslinked residues Cys-401 (23.7 Å) and Met-376 (24 Å) and the hydroxyl group of the active site Tyr-319 are indicated. A, adenine; G, guanine; C, cytosine; T, thyrosine; d, deoxyribose-phosphate.</p></figcaption></figure><p>UV XL-MS generally unambiguously distinguishes between RNA and DNA in crosslinks, because thymidine and deoxyribose crosslinks generate DNA-specific mass adducts. In contrast, many chemical crosslinks, involving purines, are ambiguous as deoxyguanosine and adenosine are isobaric (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S12</a>). The detection of adducts of two or more nucleotides harbouring cytidine, uridine, or thymidine reduces such ambiguity. Also, deoxyribose crosslinks originated unambiguously from DNA (see above). However, their presence, in particular in a cellular context, must be still considered with caution. It is not entirely clear whether crosslinking of these reactive deoxyribose derivatives might be (entirely) reversible and could lead to crosslinking to proteins not necessarily involved in direct DNA binding. In the protein TOP1, which releases negative supercoiling in DNA, we identified unambiguous UV crosslinks to deoxydinucleotides, within a peptide encompassing amino acid positions 774–788 (Fig. <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">7B</a>) [<a href="#B96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B96">96</a>]. The peptide contains Phe780 and Phe786, which are very close to the ssDNA in the 3D structure. Ambiguous crosslinks derived from chemical crosslinking (Met376, Met438, Met522, and Met669), were all located near conserved residues involved in DNA binding [<a href="#B96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B96">96</a>]. Deoxyribose crosslinks were found to Cys401 and Cys763 in the DNA- and ssDNA-binding region, respectively.</p>
<p>Overall, we identified 70 crosslinked DNA-binding transcriptional regulators (according to the Regulon database [<a href="#B97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B97">97</a>]) in <em>E. coli</em>, exclusively after chemical crosslinking. Crosslinking sites were found within or close to the described DNA-binding domains of these regulators, such as the helix–turn–helix (HTH) motif, OmpR/PhoB-type domain, or the LytTr DNA-binding domain. Among 29 transcriptional regulators with an HTH motif, in 18 proteins the crosslinking sites are not located in the HTH motif, but in N- or C-termini of the protein. These potentially novel functional regions have not yet been described as nucleic-acid binding before. Importantly, among the all (chemically) crosslinkable amino acids in transcription regulators containing HTH motifs, only a minority were crosslinked, but they were identified by multiple CSMs in XL-MS, supporting the biological/structural relevance of these identifications (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S13</a>). Owing to the ambiguity in DNA/RNA chemical crosslinking, we cannot conclude finally whether the crosslinks in the HTH domain or in other, as yet undefined, domains originated from RNA or from DNA.</p>
<p>Recently, the RNA binding properties of HTH transcription regulators have been confirmed in <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> [<a href="#B98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B98">98</a>]. According to this, we found that the MaR-type HTH domain containing transcriptional repressor MprA (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary File S1</a>) chemically crosslinked within its conserved site of the HTH domain (residues 87–121) to RNA (or DNA) and with residues at its partly disordered C- and N- termini outside the HTH domain (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S16</a>). Since MprA is not expressed in high abundance in our <em>E. coli</em> samples, it appears unlikely that the chemical crosslinking is due to a “crowding effect”, i.e. nonspecific crosslinking to RNA or DNA owing to a high abundance of the protein. Predictions of pathogenicity due to missense mutations affecting the protein structure, conducted with AlphaMissense [<a href="#B99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B99">99</a>], suggest a mild pathogenicity upon mutation in the protein termini, which points towards a possible functional interaction of these particular protein regions with RNA.</p></section></section><section id="SEC4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p>In a protein-centric analysis, we regard the detection/ identification of crosslinked amino acids by XL-MS as the most direct approach to localizing the interactions of proteins with RNA and/or DNA. Several excellent proteome-wide crosslinking studies applied UV XL-MS and obtained remarkable inventories of proteins bound to (m)RNAs [<a href="#B5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5">5–7</a>, <a href="#B13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13">13</a>, <a href="#B14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14">14</a>].</p>
<p>Compared to previous work, our study makes three major contributions: (i) establishing chemical XL-MS as a method to increase the number of crosslinks, (ii) introduce a dedicated search engine for crosslink identification and localization, and (iii) reporting the to-date largest data set of crosslinking sites for <em>E. coli</em> proteins.</p>
<p>UV crosslinking is the most frequently applied method for studying RNPs by XL-MS, while chemical XL-MS has so far been favoured for application to DNPs [<a href="#B17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17">17</a>, <a href="#B100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100">100</a>]. UV XL-MS requires, for example, 6-thio-G or purine derivatives incorporated into cellular RNAs in order to capture RNP interaction with bases other than uracil [<a href="#B3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3">3</a>, <a href="#B101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B101">101</a>]. Very recently it was demonstrated that 4-thio thymidine can be incorporated into cellular DNA, and subsequent UV irradiation followed by XL-MS revealed a large number of proteins crosslinked to DNA and allowed identification of their crosslinking sites [<a href="#B102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B102">102</a>]. We show that the reaction with alkylating chemicals, NM, diepoxybutane, and FA, offers an alternative, as guanine and adenosine are linked to proteins. We anticipate a broad application of chemical XL-MS in global interaction mapping and also in the modelling of DNA/RNA to its binding proteins in RNPs. We show that chemical crosslinking is efficient <em>in vivo</em> and <em>in vitro</em>. Compared with UV irradiation, it is relatively sensitive to the composition of the sample. For example, primary amino groups containing components in the sample, such as Tris buffer, may inhibit chemical crosslinking; other components in the cell media can also react with chemical crosslinker. In general, it is recommended to choose the optimum concentration of these crosslinkers in each <em>in vitro</em> and <em>in vivo</em> sample by titration with various crosslinker concentrations.</p>
<p>Recently, chemical, i.e. FA-based crosslinking in XL-MS studies [<a href="#B103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B103">103</a>] and also in CLIP studies, termed fCLIP [<a href="#B104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B104">104</a>] have been applied as well and shown that it extends UV crosslinking in terms of numbers of crosslinked proteins but is also highly suitable to map RNA sequences that are in contact to dsRNA-binding proteins [<a href="#B105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B105">105</a>]. Yet, it is not entirely solved whether chemical crosslinks occur mainly to primary RNA-binding proteins. It might be well the case that more indirect (“associated”) binders, which might interact with a primary RNA-binding protein chemically crosslinks to RNA in close vicinity to the primary RNA binder as well. Our own XL-MS <em>E. coli</em> data show that 501 crosslinked peptide sequences are common between UV and chemical crosslinks, revealing that chemical crosslinking also identifies primary RNA binders, if those proteins that UV crosslink to RNA are considered as primary RNA-binders based on the “zero-length” nature of UV crosslinking. Moreover, when structures of protein–dsRNA and protein–DNA complexes are available, we show here that chemicals can crosslink amino acid in contact to dsRNA and dsDNA (Figs <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">3B</a>, <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">6A</a>, and <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">7A</a>).</p>
<p>The first search engine for nucleotide–protein crosslinks, RNPxl, was restricted to UV XL-MS and required additional manual evaluation/annotation of XL-MS data [<a href="#B2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2">2</a>, <a href="#B72" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72">72</a>]. Such shortcomings have been overcome in our tool NuXL. While general-purpose peptide search engines such as MSFragger [<a href="#B106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B106">106</a>] have also been applied in several UV-XL MS studies, NuXL has been developed specifically for protein-nucleotide crosslink assignment.</p>
<p>NuXL also allows the incorporation of other protein-nucleotide crosslinkers, for example, SDA, whose NHS ester group reacts with lysine residues in RNPs and upon photoactivation forms a carbene nucleotide in close vicinity to the derivatized lysine residue (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S17A–C</a> for reaction mechanism, <em>adduct sum formulae</em>, and MS2).</p>
<p>We compared our results with results from three other UV XL-MS studies (TRAPP [<a href="#B6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6">6</a>], OOPS [<a href="#B7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7">7</a>], and RIC [<a href="#B107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B107">107</a>] of protein–RNA interactions in <em>E. coli</em>) (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S12B</a>). Our results overlap substantially with these studies (<a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S12B</a>), but our dataset additionally reveals the sites of crosslinking for all identified proteins. In addition, we detected 548 crosslinked proteins and their crosslinking sites that were not identified in previous studies. A total of 74% of these proteins were detected under only one of the four crosslinking conditions (12%, 20%, 39%, and 3% for UV, DEB, NM, and FA XL-MS, respectively; <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S12C</a>), emphasizing the advantage of the combination of UV and chemical XL-MS. For example, crosslinking sites were identified in 9 of 10 hitherto poorly characterized RBPs described in the RIC [<a href="#B107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B107">107</a>] study and in 12 of 17 putative RBPs investigated in a recent OOPS study [<a href="#B108" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B108">108</a>]; in nine of these exclusively upon chemical crosslinking.</p>
<p>Unlike in the above-mentioned studies, we did not apply a dedicated purification of RNA after crosslinking in all experiments, except for FA crosslinking of <em>E. coli</em> cells (see below). Instead, we chose a comprehensive approach aimed to enrich any (deoxy)ribonucleotide crosslink species to show the feasibility of chemical crosslinking and NuXL in RBP and DBP analysis. Based on this, particularly chemical crosslinks show ambiguities, i.e. crosslinks that could be derived from RNA, DNA, mononucleotides, or enzymatic co-factors. Also, one cannot exclude the possibility that crosslinks derived from shorter RNA oligonucleotides originated from degradation of intact RNA. Nonetheless, our data largely overlap with RNA-crosslinked proteins in other <em>E. coli</em> studies and we show that crosslinks of RNA origin coincide with nucleotide/co-factor-binding sites. Our workflow is advantageous, for comparing crosslink sites originating from RNA or DNA with assigned nucleotide/co-factor-binding sites in a single experiment. The identification of nearly all proteins of glycolysis and the TCA cycle and their crosslinking sites (with the key enzymes crosslinked by UV, DEB, NM, and FA) strengthen the view that metabolic enzymes are moonlighting RNA-binding proteins, as previously reported in pro- and eukaryotes [<a href="#B86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B86">86</a>, <a href="#B87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B87">87</a>]. For FA XL-MS of <em>E. coli</em> cells we applied a silica-based enrichment strategy of intact (crosslinked) RNA (see the ‘Materials and methods’ section). The reason for this was that upon application of our general XL-MS workflow the yield of FA-crosslinked peptide-(oligo)nucleotide conjugates was surprisingly low (for which we do not yet have a compelling explanation). Importantly, this particular dataset, which shows crosslinked metabolic enzymes, strongly indicates that these must have derived from intact RNA rather than from single nucleotides or co-factors due to the presence of crosslinked dinucleotide.</p>
<p>Alkylating reagents such as those used in this study form intra- and interstrand crosslinks in DNA and also connect bases in RNA [<a href="#B109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B109">109</a>]. We can only speculate that such crosslinks theoretically influence chemical protein–RNA crosslinking if it occurs within sites where the proteins bind and hence would crosslink. Such a scenario would also occur in protein–DNA crosslinking.</p>
<p>In this study, we could not (yet) systematically compare DNA and RNA crosslinking (owing to the ambiguous nature of possible A and dG crosslinking), including nucleotide crosslink reactivity. Our statistical analysis shows that RNA-binding proteins are highly abundant in <em>E. coli</em> cells in accordance with the abundance distribution of RNA and DNA (20% and 3% of the cell’s dry mass, respectively) [<a href="#B110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B110">110</a>]. Accordingly, we demonstrated a statistically significant enrichment towards the putative RNA-binding sites on primary and tertiary protein structure levels, but not for DNA-binding sites (Fig. <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">5</a>A and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S11</a>). Hence, we infer that, at least under the growth conditions applied in this study, i.e. growing and crosslinking <em>E. coli</em> in nutrient-rich media where cells strongly allocate their resources to transcription and translation proteins are more prone to crosslinking with RNA than with DNA.</p></section><section id="sec40"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Material</h2>

</div></div></section></section><section id="_adda93_" lang="en" class="data-availability-statement"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data Availability Statement</h3>
<p>The datasets (.raw, .mzML, .idxML, and .txt files) generated and analysed for this study are deposited in the MassIVE repository and submitted to ProteomeXchange Consortium [<a href="#B118" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B118">118</a>] and are available using the following identifiers MSV000094879 (MassIVE), PXD052614 (ProteomeXchange). Dnmt2–tRNA<sup>Asp</sup> models and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S5</a> and <a href="#sup1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Fig. S11</a> were deposited via figshare and are available under the following link: <a href="https://figshare.com/s/6b2091a24a327ed546e4" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://figshare.com/s/6b2091a24a327ed546e4</a>.</p></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
